## Endnotes / 译注

===Original===
1. These cyamos-leaves were actually used for kitchen-utensils by the people of Alexandria; and their sale provided a regular livelihood. ↩︎
2. Stone wells on the banks of the Nile, in which the water rose and fell as in the river itself; marked columns indicated the maximum, minimum and middle gauge. Inspectors informed the people beforehand how high the Nile would rise and when the stream would be likely to overflow its banks. ↩︎
3. Caleb’s description of Saba owes very little to the author’s invention. Nearly all these details upon Arabia Felix will be found set forth in Strabo’s Geography. ↩︎

===Chinese===
1. 亚历山大里亚的居民确实用这种基阿莫斯树叶（cyamos-leaves）制作厨房用具；贩卖它们足以维持一份稳定的生计。 ↩︎
2. 尼罗河岸上的石井，井中之水如河水本身一样涨落；柱上标有刻度，指示最高、最低与中间水位。监察人员会预先告知民众尼罗河将涨到多高、河水何时可能漫过堤岸。 ↩︎
3. 卡莱布对萨巴的描述几乎全无作者的虚构。关于幸福阿拉伯（Arabia Felix）的这些细节，几乎都可详见斯特拉波（Strabo）的《地理学》。 ↩︎

===Original===
4. The Red Sea. ↩︎
5. The old canal through the Isthmus of Suez. ↩︎
6. Here follow the hour, day and month. ↩︎

===Chinese===
4. 即红海。 ↩︎
5. 即穿过苏伊士地峡的古运河。 ↩︎
6. 此处原文接下去是时、日、月。 ↩︎

## I / 一

===Original===
The night that hung over the sea was windless and blissfully silver-pure after the glowing splendour of the day; and the great quadrireme glided evenly and softly, as though upon a lake, under a wide firmament of stars. The thin horizon was purely outlined around the oval sea; and on this wide world there was nothing but the stars and the ship.

But the ship resounded with music. There was the constantly repeated melodious phrase of the three hundred rowers, soft and monotone, in a melancholy minor, with ever the same refrain, after which the boatswain gave out the chant, after which the chorus of rowers again threw back their long, hushed phrase of melancholy, the soft, monotonous accompaniment of the wearying work, the musical encouragement to repeat the same movement of the arms and the same bending of the body over the loins.

This music rose in a mournful swell from the ship’s lower deck and harmonizing with it was the soft stroke of the oars, which were like the legs of some graceful sea-animal; the ship herself, with her swanlike raised prow, suggested an elegant monster swimming through the lake-calm waters of that silvery night-world, a monster with a swan’s neck and hundreds of slender, evenly-moving legs and winged with two rose-yellow sails, which rose and bellied gently at the ship’s own motion, but did not swell, because the wind lay still.

While the great, winged navigium glided upon that harmony of slaves’ song and oar-strokes, there came from the rear half-deck the blither song of the sailors idling after their work. It sounded cheerful with deep, bass male voices, without the rowers’ melancholy; and there was one sailor who gave the time in a higher voice, for the seamen were at liberty to sing, but their singing must be artistically led, because melodious music meant a prosperous voyage and averted evil chances and did not let the shrill voices of the sirens ring from under the waters and because the pure sound of the human voice kept away the rocks drifting under the sea and compelled the sea-serpent to dive back into the deep.

And through these two choirs, through the melancholy singing of the rowers and the jubilant seamen’s song, a delicate female voice let fall clear, love-yearning notes, always with a playful and wanton final phrase. It was—while as it were golden beads tinkled from twanged harp-strings: those very bright gold beads which tinkle from the strings of the little four-stringed Lesbian harp—a hymn to the goddess Aphrodite, whose name constantly rang back, plaintively and wantonly, in the singer’s Greek, exotically soft against the harder Latin of the men’s joy-song and the melodious melancholy phrase of the lower deck. …

===Chinese===
白日的辉煌灿烂过后，笼罩海面的夜无风无浪，银一般纯净，纯净得令人幸福；巨大的四列桨船平稳而轻柔地滑行着，仿佛行在一片湖泊之上，头顶是繁星铺满的辽阔天穹。纤薄的地平线在椭圆的海四周勾出纯净的轮廓；在这广阔的天地之间，除了星辰与船，别无他物。

然而船上乐声回荡。那是三百名桨手反复吟唱的旋律，轻柔而单调，是一支忧郁的小调，副歌始终如一：水手长领起号子，桨手们的合唱便又扬起那悠长而低抑的忧郁乐句——这软软的单音，是疲累劳作的伴奏，是以乐声劝勉众人重复同样的挥臂、同样的俯身弓腰。

乐声从下层甲板哀哀涌起，与它相和的是桨叶轻柔的起落，那一排排桨宛如某种优雅海兽的腿脚；而这船本身，扬着天鹅般高昂的船首，恰似一头优雅的巨怪，游过银色夜世界里湖面般平静的水域——一头生着天鹅颈项、数百条细长匀动之腿的巨怪，张着两面玫瑰黄色的帆翼。帆随着船自身的运动轻轻鼓起，却并不涨满，因为风已经止息。

当这艘张着帆翼的大船就这样和着奴隶的歌声与桨的起落滑行时，后半层甲板上传来水手们歇工闲坐后更欢快的歌声。深沉的男低音听着喜气洋洋，没有桨手的那种忧郁；有一个水手用较高的嗓音打着拍子，因为海员尽可放声歌唱，但歌必须唱得有章法：悦耳的音乐预示航程顺遂，能避开凶险，不让海妖尖利的歌声从水底响起；人声的清纯之音能驱开水下漂移的礁石，逼得海蛇重新潜回深渊。

而穿过这两队合唱——穿过桨手的哀歌与水手的欢唱——一个清细的女声洒下清亮、渴恋的音符，收尾总带着一句俏皮而妖娆的拖腔。那是——仿佛有金珠从拨响的琴弦上叮咚坠落：正是那种从莱斯博斯小竖琴四根弦上滚落的、异常明亮的金珠——一首献给女神阿佛洛狄忒（Aphrodite）的颂歌。歌者的希腊语里，女神之名带着哀怨与冶艳不断回响，比起男人们欢歌中较为硬朗的拉丁语、比起下层甲板那旋律化的忧郁乐句，它显得异样地柔软。……

===Original===
Publius Lucius Sabinus lay on the prow in a pavilion of Tyrian red-silk curtains and listened. The music sounding up from his ship in the silver-pure, windless night, through the blissful, wide-pure, star-strewn air, brought him a moment’s respite from grief. He lay calmer now, sated with despair, with his soul of sorrow as it were bathed in the melodious music. He stared, as though without thinking, now almost free from grief, at the silver statue of Aphrodite, the patroness of his ship, in front of which an alabaster lamp burned, while a light spiral of nard curled around the goddess’ feet from an incense-boat.

It was not possible to feel always, always, the same vehement grief. Tomorrow, nay, in an hour, the sorrow would resume its violence; now, in this night of coolness and melody, there was just a brief rest, a moment of annihilation, almost a sense of wistful well-being. And, in this calmer mood, Lucius felt a need to speak a friendly word to his old friend and tutor, as he had not done since the voyage began.

He struck the gong beside his couch; and a little black slave appeared.

“Tarrar,” said Lucius, “find Thrasyllus for me and tell him that I await him.”

The little Libyan slave, looking like a monkey in his scanty, many-coloured coat, made a drolly serious movement of reverence, crept backwards and disappeared. It was not long before he lifted the hanging and Thrasyllus stepped into the presence of his young master, Publius Lucius Sabinus.

===Chinese===
普布利乌斯·卢基乌斯·萨比努斯（Publius Lucius Sabinus）躺在船首一座垂着推罗红绸帷幔的凉棚里，听着。在这银色纯净、无风的夜里，从他船上涌起的音乐，穿过那幸福而澄澈、繁星四撒的夜气，给他悲恸的心片刻喘息。他此刻安静了些，绝望已经餍足，忧伤的灵魂仿佛浸浴在乐声之中。他近乎无思无虑地凝望着银铸的阿佛洛狄忒像——他这条船的守护女神——像前燃着一盏雪花石膏灯，一缕淡淡的甘松香从香炉里升起，绕着女神的双足袅袅盘旋。

人不可能永远、永远怀着同样剧烈的悲痛。明天，不，就在一小时之后，悲伤便会重新肆虐；此刻，在这个清凉而有乐声的夜里，只有短暂的歇息，一刻的寂灭，几乎是一种怅惘的安宁。在这较平静的心境里，卢基乌斯觉得需要向他的老友兼师父说句亲热话——启航以来他还从未这样过。

他敲了敲卧榻旁的铜锣；一个小黑奴应声出现。

“塔拉尔（Tarrar），”卢基乌斯说，“去把特拉叙卢斯（Thrasyllus）找来，告诉他我在等他。”

那利比亚小奴穿着他那件窄小斑斓的短衣，活像一只猴子；他行了一个滑稽而郑重的礼，倒退着蹭出去，不见了。不多时，他掀开门帘，特拉叙卢斯走进来，来到他年轻的主人普布利乌斯·卢基乌斯·萨比努斯面前。

===Original===
The pedagogue, or tutor, was an elderly freedman, tall, lean, serious, grey-haired and grey-bearded. His eyes were kindly; his mouth wore a fatherly smile.

Lucius, without rising, stretched out his hand to him:

“Thrasyllus,” he said, “forgive me if I have been unkind.”

This was all that he said. His voice sounded deep, manly and earnest. The old tutor had taken a seat on a footstool beside his master’s couch. And, holding the other’s hand for a moment in his own, he said:

“Lucius, I thank you for that word. But I have nothing to forgive, dear young master. You are the master, I am your slave, your slave still, even though you have given me my freedom. I am your servant, but one who has fatherly feelings for you. I feel a father’s love towards you; and you have never forbidden it. It is well; and I am content. I serve you and I love you. But I thank you for that generous word. That is what you are: generous, just. You are far above all pride. You know how to admit when you are wrong. And I, on my side, if you think that you need it, gladly grant you my forgiveness, though the word is unsuited to my mouth. You were bitter and you were suffering: your sorrow drove you mad. Your nature is violent in all things: in your love, in your sorrow, in your hatred, in all your passions and angers. …”

===Chinese===
这位师父，或者说家庭教师，是位年长的获释奴，高瘦、严肃，须发皆灰。他目光慈和，嘴角挂着父辈的微笑。

卢基乌斯没有起身，向他伸出手去：

“特拉叙卢斯，”他说，“倘若我有什么不好，请原谅我。”

他只说了这一句。嗓音低沉、雄浑而恳切。老教师已在主人卧榻旁的脚凳上坐下，把对方的手在自己手里握了片刻，说道：

“卢基乌斯，我谢谢你这句话。可我没有什么要原谅的，亲爱的少主人。您是主人，我是您的奴隶，纵然您已赐我自由，我仍是您的奴隶。我是您的仆人，一个对您怀着父爱的仆人。我对您怀着为父之爱；您也从不曾禁止。这样很好；我心安然。我侍奉您，我爱您。但我谢谢您这句宽厚的话。您就是这样的人：宽厚，公正。您远在一切骄傲之上。您懂得认错。至于我，若您觉得需要，我也欣然给您我的宽恕，尽管这个词不合我的口。您心里苦，您在受苦：您的悲伤把您逼疯了。您的天性事事剧烈：爱也剧烈，悲也剧烈，恨也剧烈，一切激情与怒火无不如此。……”

===Original===
“I was not generous and not just, Thrasyllus, and I raised my hand against you. Forgive me.”

The old tutor shrugged his shoulders:

“I forgive you, I forgive you. Your blood flows hotly and the red cloud sometimes blinds you. Certainly you must control and master yourself. But I, I am your slave, though I feel for you like a father; and that you raised your hand against me: what of it? It was a movement of anger. You are as mettlesome as a young colt. And sorrow drove you mad.”

“It does so still. Sometimes, sometimes it is as though I felt a fury of frenzy here, inside me, in my breast! Then I *must* have her, have her back, have her here, beside me, in my arms, at my breast, at my lips! O ye gods, ye gods, ye gods!”

He drew a deep breath, moaned and sobbed.

===Chinese===
“我既不宽厚也不公正，特拉叙卢斯，我还举手打了你。原谅我。”

老教师耸了耸肩：

“我原谅您，我原谅您。您的血滚热，那片红雾有时会蒙住您的眼睛。您当然必须克制自己、驾驭自己。可我，我是您的奴隶，虽说我对您像对儿子；您举手打了我，又算得了什么？那不过是一时之怒。您烈性得像匹小马驹。是悲伤把您逼疯了。”

“它现在还在逼我。有时候，有时候我仿佛觉得胸中这里，我身体里面，涌起一阵疯狂的狂怒！那时我*必须*得到她，把她找回来，让她在这里，在我身边，在我怀里，贴着我的胸，贴着我的唇！哦，众神啊，众神啊，众神啊！”

他深深吸了一口气，呻吟着，抽噎起来。

===Original===
“Be still, dear young master,” said the tutor. “Try to forget and try to be resigned. She is gone. She is not to be found. We have searched everywhere. You have vainly squandered treasures to find her. Ilia is gone. It is three months now since she disappeared. She was probably kidnapped by pirates while bathing. She used often to bathe in the sea, among the rocks. …”

“Is the villa at Baiae sold? I *won’t* go back to it! … Since she is no longer there, since she has disappeared, disappeared! She has disappeared! She has disappeared without a trace! Just one sandal on the shore. It was a calm sea. She cannot have been drowned! … In my house she was queen! My Ilia: she was the queen of my house, though she was a slave! Everything for her and because of her! She was my slave, but she had slaves herself, male and female: she had the jewels of an empress, she had the raiment of a goddess! I worshipped her as I would Venus herself! And she has disappeared, she has disappeared without a trace, without a trace! Not a thing of hers has been found save a sandal, a sandal! Where can she be? Is she dead, is she alive? Did she run away, was she kidnapped, has she been murdered? Shall I never, never see her again? Here, here”—he rose suddenly—“here, in my boiling breast, I feel it welling up now, the fury of frenzy! I want her, I will have her! Ilia, Ilia, Ilia!”

And he uttered a despairing cry, a scream of anguish, and burst into sobs.

His cry, his scream was heard in the night, throughout the ship.

And suddenly, because of his grief, all the music fell silent: the melancholy chant of the rowers, the joy-song of the sailors and the hymn to the goddess, sung to the twanging Lesbian harp.

Only the oars continued to beat the waves. For the rest, silence, silence, silence … over all the ship, under the starry dome. …

===Chinese===
“安静些，亲爱的少主人，”教师说。“试着忘掉，试着顺从命运。她走了。她找不到了。我们哪里都找遍了。您为寻她白白掷去了多少财宝。伊利娅（Ilia）走了。她失踪已经三个月了。多半是她在洗浴时被海盗劫走了。她从前常在海里洗浴，在礁石中间。……”

“巴伊亚（Baiae）的别墅卖掉了吗？我*不*回那儿去！……既然她不在那里了，既然她失踪了，失踪了！她失踪了！她无影无踪地失踪了！岸边只留下一只凉鞋。那天海面平静。她不可能淹死！……在我家里她是王后！我的伊利娅：她是我家的王后，虽然她是个奴隶！一切都为她、都因她！她是我的奴隶，可她自己也有奴隶，男女都有：她有皇后的珠宝，她有女神的衣裳！我敬她如敬维纳斯（Venus）本人！而她失踪了，她无影无踪地失踪了，无影无踪！她的物件一件也没找到，只有一只凉鞋，一只凉鞋！她能在哪里？她死了，还是活着？她是逃走了，是被劫了，还是被谋害了？难道我永远、永远见不着她了？这里，这里”——他霍地站起——“这里，在我沸腾的胸中，我感到它正在涌上来，那疯狂的狂怒！我要她，我一定要得到她！伊利娅，伊利娅，伊利娅！”

他发出一声绝望的喊叫，一声痛苦的尖号，随即放声痛哭。

他的喊声、他的尖号在夜里传开，传遍全船。

突然，因为他的悲痛，一切乐声都沉寂了：桨手忧郁的号子，水手的欢歌，还有和着铮铮的莱斯博斯竖琴唱给女神的颂歌。

只有桨还在继续击水。其余的一切，静默，静默，静默……笼罩全船，在星空的穹顶之下。……

===Original===
Then the boatswain’s voice made itself heard. The rowers’ melodious phrase rose in a mournful swell, always the same. And the high voice of the sailor who led the singing set the time. The seamen took up the chant. And bright, golden beads from the four-stringed harp fell like clear drops through the night; and the Greek hymn of the songstress pined away with love and tenderness, to ring out suddenly, imploringly:

“Aphrodite! … Aphrodite! …”

And he uttered a despairing cry, a scream of anguish, and burst into sobs.

“Aphrodite! … Aphrodite! …”

===Chinese===
随后水手长的嗓音响了起来。桨手们的旋律哀哀涌起，一如既往。领唱水手的高音定下了拍子。海员们接起了号子。四弦竖琴上明亮的金珠如清脆的水滴洒落夜空；女歌手的希腊颂歌在爱与温柔中憔悴消融，忽而又响起来，声声哀恳：

“阿佛洛狄忒！……阿佛洛狄忒！……”

他发出一声绝望的喊叫，一声痛苦的尖号，随即放声痛哭。

“阿佛洛狄忒！……阿佛洛狄忒！……”

## II / 二

===Original===
Lucius lay on his cushions sobbing like a child. Beside him sat old Thrasyllus, with his hand on his master’s heaving shoulder:

“Lucius, pray control yourself,” he said. “Master yourself and yield piously to Fate. Ilia is gone, she is gone. She is probably gone forever. She has disappeared. Pirates must have kidnapped her while she was bathing. … Do not think of her any more. Life is rich in promise. Fortune has favoured you not only with untold treasures, but also with genius and soul. You love beauty and study, every art and every science. You did well to follow my advice at last and not to go on languishing with grief in the villa at Baiae. Yes, it is sold. We shall never go back there. The villa is sold to Caesar. For almost nothing. Tiberius can look upon it as a gift! What does it matter? Forget the villa and … forget Ilia. … We are now sailing towards Egypt, the birthplace of all wisdom, the cradle of humanity. You did well to follow my advice: you needed distraction, my dear young master; and this distraction will bring healing to your sick soul. Tomorrow we shall reach Alexandria. The voyage is auspicious and will probably be completed without storms. Try to sleep now; and, once again, thank you for your kind word. You are generous. I had nothing to forgive, but I am grateful that you love me better than you would a simple slave. Good night. Good night, Lucius.”

===Chinese===
卢基乌斯躺在软垫上，像个孩子似的抽泣。老特拉叙卢斯坐在他身旁，一只手放在主人起伏的肩头上：

“卢基乌斯，请克制自己，”他说。“管住自己，虔诚地顺从命运吧。伊利娅去了，她去了。多半是永远地去了。她失踪了。一定是她洗浴时被海盗劫走了。……别再想她了。生活多的是指望。命运女神厚待您，不光给了您数不清的财宝，还给了您才情与灵魂。您爱美与学问，爱一切艺术、一切科学。您终于听了我的劝，没有继续在巴伊亚的别墅里为悲伤憔悴下去，这样做很对。是的，别墅卖了。我们再不回那儿去了。别墅卖给了恺撒。几乎等于白送。提比略（Tiberius）大可以把它当作一份礼物！有什么要紧？忘掉那别墅，也……忘掉伊利娅吧。……我们如今正驶向埃及（Egypt），那一切智慧的发祥地、人类的摇篮。您听了我的劝做得对：您需要散心，亲爱的少主人；这番散心会医治您病了的灵魂。明天我们就到亚历山大里亚（Alexandria）了。这趟航程吉顺，多半会一路无风无浪。现在试着睡吧；还有，再一次谢谢您那句亲热话。您真宽厚。我本没有什么要原谅的，但您爱我胜过爱一个寻常的奴隶，我很感激。晚安。晚安，卢基乌斯。”

===Original===
The tutor left the pavilion:

“Draw the curtains close, Tarrar,” he said to the Libyan boy. “Noiselessly.”

“Yes, Thrasyllus,” said the child.

The tutor walked to the end of the long deck. The sailors’ song was hushed, the hymn was hushed; only the rowers’ melancholy phrase sounded very softly, muffled in undertone.

===Chinese===
教师出了凉棚：

“把帘子拉严，塔拉尔，”他对那利比亚男孩说。“不许出声。”

“是，特拉叙卢斯，”孩子说。

教师沿着长长的甲板走到尽头。水手的歌声静了，颂歌也静了；只有桨手们忧郁的乐句还幽幽响着，压成低低的暗音。

===Original===
The old man stopped. On a pile of cushions lay Catullus, Lucius’ penniless uncle, potbellied as Silenus and with a bald and shining pate; and on a low chair sat Cora, the Greek slave from Cos. Her harp stood like a rounded bow by her side; and she leant her head against it.

“Well, Thrasyllus,” mumbled Catullus, sleepily, “how goes it with my nephew?”

“He has spoken a kind word to me,” replied the tutor, joyfully.

“A kind word?” cried Catullus, raising himself, with his hands still behind the grey fringe of his cranium. “I shall become jealous! I have not had a kind word since that wench bolted. …”

“Ssh! Be silent, worthy Catullus,” said Thrasyllus. “He believes that she has been kidnapped. Leave him in that belief.”

“And everyone knows—the steersman told me so himself—that she ran away with Carus the Cypriote, the sailor! Everyone knows it, all the sailors and rowers. …”

===Chinese===
老人停住脚。一堆软垫上躺着卡图卢斯（Catullus）——卢基乌斯那位一贫如洗的叔父——肚子滚圆，像西勒诺斯（Silenus），秃顶油亮；一张矮椅上坐着科拉（Cora），来自科斯岛的希腊女奴。她的竖琴立在她手边，像一张弯弓；她把头靠在琴上。

“喂，特拉叙卢斯，”卡图卢斯睡意朦胧地咕哝道，“我的侄儿怎么样了？”

“他跟我说了一句亲热话，”教师喜滋滋地回答。

“一句亲热话？”卡图卢斯喊起来，直起身子，双手还垫在颅骨那圈灰发后面。“我要吃醋了！自打那小蹄子跑了，我就没得过一句亲热话。……”

“嘘！别作声，可敬的卡图卢斯，”特拉叙卢斯说。“他相信她是被劫走的。就让他这么信着吧。”

“可谁都知道——舵手亲口告诉我的——她是跟那个水手、塞浦路斯人卡鲁斯（Carus）私奔的！谁都知道，水手桨手全都知道。……”

===Original===
“Ssh!” Thrasyllus repeated. “Never tell him! He worshipped the woman and she was not worth it! She reigned as queen in his house … and she ran away with Carus the Cypriote! She left a master like Lucius for a scoundrel like Carus!”

“And Lucius still believes that Venus watches over him!”

“Why should the goddess not watch over him, my Lord Catullus? Ilia was not worthy of Lucius: the goddess was in very truth watching over Lucius when she aroused that mad passion in Ilia. Who knows what great and high happiness she has in store for him in the future?”

“I don’t believe in the gods, Thrasyllus, not even in Bacchus,” said Catullus. “You know I don’t. Since the gods ordained that I should be born as poor as a rat and my nephew surrounded by every earthly treasure, since … since I was a babe at the breast, I have not believed in the gods! And least of all in Venus … though I could almost begin to believe in her when Cora sings to her as she has been doing.”

The Greek slave raised her head from the harp on which she was leaning:

===Chinese===
“嘘！”特拉叙卢斯又说。“千万别告诉他！他把那女人当神拜，她可不配！她在他家里称王称后……却跟塞浦路斯人卡鲁斯跑了！她撇下卢基乌斯这样的主人，跟了卡鲁斯那样的无赖！”

“而卢基乌斯还当维纳斯在护佑着他呢！”

“女神为什么不该护佑他呢，卡图卢斯老爷？伊利娅配不上卢基乌斯：女神在伊利娅心里撩起那阵疯狂情欲的时候，其实正是在护佑卢基乌斯。谁知道她将来还给他存着多大的幸福呢？”

“我不信神，特拉叙卢斯，连巴库斯（Bacchus）都不信，”卡图卢斯说。“你知道我不信。既然诸神安排我生下来穷得像只老鼠，而我侄儿却被人间的一切财宝团团围住，打从……打从我还是吃奶的娃娃起，我就不信神了！最不信的就是维纳斯……不过，科拉像方才那样对着她歌唱时，我几乎又要信起她来。”

那希腊女奴从她倚着的竖琴上抬起头来：

===Original===
“Did I sing well?” she asked. “Thrasyllus, did I sing well?”

“Very well indeed, Cora,” said Thrasyllus.

“Did *he* say anything about my song?”

“No,” said Thrasyllus, “he did not.”

“Has he *never* said anything about my singing?”

“No, Cora; he is suffering too much to take notice of it.”

===Chinese===
“我唱得好吗？”她问。“特拉叙卢斯，我唱得好吗？”

“好得很，科拉，”特拉叙卢斯说。

“*他*说起过我的歌吗？”

“没有，”特拉叙卢斯说，“他没有。”

“他*从来不*曾说起过我的歌唱吗？”

“没有，科拉；他痛苦太深，顾不上留心这个。”

===Original===
“Poor Cora!” said Catullus. “She has been singing hymns to Aphrodite for three months now, ever since Ilia went away and since you, Thrasyllus, bought Cora for her beautiful voice, to divert Lucius a little; and I believe that Lucius has not even observed that Cora can sing… much less realized that she exists!”

“It doesn’t matter,” said the Greek slave, leaning her head against the harp again.

Catullus yawned and puffed out his stomach:

“I shall stay and sleep here in my cushions,” he said. “I shall not go to my pavilion. I shall stay and sleep here, under the stars. Tomorrow we shall be at Alexandria! Alexandria! The city with the most exquisite cooking, so they say! I am tired of Rome and Baiae; I am really tired of roast peacock and oysters. Nothing but Rome and roast peacock; nothing but Baiae and oysters: I shall end by turning into a peacock or an oyster! Change of diet is the secret of good health. I was losing my gaiety and had not a joke left in me to charm an occasional laugh out of Lucius. He did not even listen to *me*, Cora, when I was witty… and you expect him to listen to your song! He listens to nothing and nobody since Ilia is gone.”

“Was she so very beautiful?” asked Cora.

“She was very beautiful,” said Thrasyllus, with grave appreciation.

===Chinese===
“可怜的科拉！”卡图卢斯说。“伊利娅走后，自打你特拉叙卢斯为她那副好嗓子买下科拉、好给卢基乌斯解解闷起，她唱阿佛洛狄忒的颂歌已经唱了三个月了；可我相信卢基乌斯压根儿没觉察到科拉会唱歌……更别说意识到她的存在了！”

“不要紧，”希腊女奴说着，又把头靠回竖琴上。

卡图卢斯打了个哈欠，鼓起肚子：

“我就留在这儿，睡我自己的软垫，”他说。“我不回我的凉棚去了。我就留在这儿睡，睡在星星底下。明天我们就到亚历山大里亚了！亚历山大里亚！据说那是烹调最考究的城！罗马和巴伊亚我腻了；烤孔雀和牡蛎我是真吃腻了。除了罗马就是烤孔雀；除了巴伊亚就是牡蛎：我早晚要变成一只孔雀或者一只牡蛎！换换口味是养生之道。我的快活劲儿正在流失，肚子里连一句俏皮话都不剩，逗不出卢基乌斯偶尔一笑了。我耍机锋的时候他连*我*都不听，科拉……你还指望他听你唱歌！伊利娅走后，他什么都不听，谁的话都不听。”

“她真有那么美吗？”科拉问。

“她非常美，”特拉叙卢斯郑重而赞赏地说。

===Original===
“She was beautiful,” Catullus echoed, in airy praise, “but she was too heavy and too big. Her ankles were not slender. Her wrists were as thick as a man’s.”

“She was *very* beautiful,” Thrasyllus repeated. “She was as beautiful as a goddess.”

“That is just where I never agreed,” cried Catullus, vehemently, “either with you or with my nephew. You both said that she was like a goddess. …”

“She was like the Cnidian Venus of Praxiteles,” Thrasyllus persisted.

“I could never see it!” Catullus persisted, in his turn. “I could never see it. There may have been something of Praxiteles’ Venus in the lines of her body… something, perhaps, though much coarser; but her face certainly lacked the charm, the smile of that divine statue. Now, though I do not believe in the gods, though I do not believe in Venus, I do believe in my own correct and sometimes sober opinion! I was not in love with Ilia as Thrasyllus and Lucius were! And really, between ourselves, I can understand her bolting, though she did reign as queen in the house. She was far too much admired for her divine ankles and wrists and for her big feet and hands! Did she not sometimes have to turn and turn for an hour, while Lucius lay looking at her, to turn on a revolving pedestal, which two slaves under the floor moved round and round and round, and did not Lucius grow angry if she stirred? ‘I can’t endure this, uncle!’ she would often declare to me; and I can well understand it. To play at being a living statue strikes me as wearisome; and I also should say, ‘Thank you for nothing,’ if my nephew were to take it into his head, because nature has at least blessed me with a fairly perfect form, to make me turn and turn on a revolving pedestal as Cupid with his bow and arrow or as Ganymede with a drinking-cup in his hand! What do *you* say, dreamy Cora?”

“I don’t know,” said Cora. “No one will ask me to pose as the Cnidian Venus. I have nothing but my voice. …”

===Chinese===
“她是美，”卡图卢斯随声附和，夸得轻飘飘的，“可她太沉、太大了。她的脚踝不细。手腕跟男人的一样粗。”

“她*非常*美，”特拉叙卢斯又说了一遍。“她美得像一位女神。”

“这正是我从来不同意的地方，”卡图卢斯激烈地嚷道，“不同意你，也不同意我侄儿。你们俩都说她像位女神。……”

“她像普拉克西特列斯（Praxiteles）雕的克尼多斯的维纳斯，”特拉叙卢斯坚持道。

“我就是从来瞧不出！”卡图卢斯也针锋相对地坚持。“我就是从来瞧不出。她身体的线条里兴许是有几分普拉克西特列斯那尊维纳斯的味道……有那么几分，也许，只是粗糙得多；可她的脸蛋儿肯定缺少那尊神像的韵味、那神像的微笑。如今呢，我虽然不信神，不信维纳斯，我倒信我自己的、有时还颇为清醒的正确见解！我可不像特拉叙卢斯和卢基乌斯那样爱着伊利娅！说真的，咱们私下里讲，她那场私奔我完全能理解，虽说她在家里称王称后。她那双神明似的脚踝手腕、那双大手大脚，受的崇拜也太多了！难道她有时不得不来来回回转上一个钟头，让卢基乌斯躺着观赏——站在一座旋转座上，地板底下两个奴隶推着那座儿一圈又一圈地转——而她只要动一动，卢基乌斯不就要发火吗？‘这个我受不了，叔父！’她常对我这样宣称；我太能理解了。让我扮一尊活雕像，我觉得腻味透了；倘若我侄儿哪天起了念头——造化至少还赏了我一副相当完美的身段——要我也站上旋转座，扮成持弓搭箭的丘比特（Cupid），或者手擎酒杯的伽倪墨得斯（Ganymede）转来转去，我也会说一句：‘多谢好意，恕不奉陪！’*你*说呢，爱做梦的科拉？”

“我不知道，”科拉说。“没人会请我去扮克尼多斯的维纳斯。我除了嗓子，一无所有。……”

===Original===
“And I nothing but a terrible sleepy feeling!” yawned Catullus. “I shall stay and sleep here, under the stars. …”

He stretched himself and heaved his body over; two slaves approached and covered him carefully with silken sheets and woollen blankets and pushed pillows under his head, his loins and his feet. He accepted their attentions like a child. And, when he had turned over, he at once fell asleep like a child, with not a wrinkle of care in his bald forehead, which shone like ivory in the soft light of the stars.

Cora had risen to her feet:

“Good night, Thrasyllus,” she said.

“Good night, Cora,” said the tutor, paternally.

===Chinese===
“我除了困得要命，也一无所有！”卡图卢斯打着哈欠说。“我就留在这儿睡，睡在星星底下。……”

他伸展四肢，笨重地翻过身去；两个奴隶走上前来，仔细地给他盖上绸单和毛毯，往他头下、腰下、脚下塞枕头。他像个孩子似的领受着他们的伺候。一翻过身，他立刻又像个孩子似的睡着了，秃顶的额头上没有一丝忧虑的皱纹，那额头在星光柔和的清辉里亮得像象牙。

科拉站起身来：

“晚安，特拉叙卢斯，”她说。

“晚安，科拉，”教师父亲般地说。

===Original===
The Greek slave, her harp tucked into her arm, moved away slowly. She lifted the hanging of a cabin which she shared above-deck with some other slaves. These were sleeping in six or seven narrow beds close together. A rose-coloured lantern shed a vague glimmer, here over a hip rounded in sleep, there over a face with shut eyes, framed in black tresses and white, raised arms.

The slave undressed in silence. Her muslin peplos woven with gold flowers fell from her. She stood naked. She looked at her wrists, which were slim, like a patrician’s. She stooped and looked at her ankles. She arched the instep of her narrow, shapely foot. And she passed her slender fingers over her hips, which were like a virgin’s, and over her waist, round which she could almost make her two hands meet. Then she took up a metal hand-mirror and looked at herself in the light of the rose-coloured lantern. She half-closed her big eyes, which were like gigantic sapphires in mother-o’-pearl shells, very soft, very bright, very big, with the streak of antimony stretching to the temples. Then she smiled.

But next she gave a very deep sigh. She lay down on her little narrow bed between two other beds. A slave had moved slightly in her sleep, muttering. Cora drew a sheet over herself; and her great eyes stared, without seeing, into the rose-coloured lantern.

In the windless night the ship glided over the sea, which was calm as a lake; and there was nothing but the beating of the oars and the lulling melodious phrase of the rowers. …

===Chinese===
希腊女奴把竖琴夹在臂弯里，缓缓走开了。她掀起一间舱室的门帘——在甲板上她与另外几个女奴同住这间舱。她们睡在六七张紧挨着的窄床上。一盏玫瑰色的灯笼洒下朦胧的微光，这儿照着一具睡梦中圆润的胯，那儿照着一张阖眼的脸，黑发环绕，双臂雪白地扬着。

女奴默默脱衣。她那织着金花的平纹细布佩普罗斯从身上滑落。她赤裸着站立。她看自己的手腕，纤细如世家贵女。她俯下身看自己的脚踝。她窄而秀气的脚背拱起。她修长的手指抚过少女般的胯，抚过腰肢——那腰细得她双手几乎可以合围。随后她拿起一面金属手镜，就着玫瑰色灯笼的光照自己。她半阖上大眼睛——那双眼像珍珠母贝壳里两颗巨大的蓝宝石，非常柔、非常亮、非常大，锑粉描出的细线一直延到鬓角。然后她笑了。

可紧接着她深深地叹了口气。她躺到自己那张窄小的床上，夹在另外两张床中间。一个女奴在睡梦里动了动，咕哝着什么。科拉把一条单子拉到身上；她的大眼睛怔怔地望着那盏玫瑰色的灯笼，什么也没看见。

无风的夜里，船滑过湖面般平静的海；除了桨声与桨手们催眠般的旋律，万籁俱寂。……

===Original===
Sometimes … a singsong order from the steersman, up in his lookout turret. …

And then a creaking of heavy ropes over great pulleys. …

===Chinese===
有时……是高处瞭望塔上舵手一句拉腔拉调的号令。……

接着是粗重的缆绳碾过巨大滑轮的吱嘎声。……

## III / 三

===Original===
Next morning, the soft, even light of a tea-rose dawn spread over a magic spectacle, beautiful as a marvellous dream, flimsy as a vision, compelling as an enchantment. The ship had glided past the monumental, marble, nine-storeyed Pharos into the Great Harbour; and Alexandria lay before the eyes of the delighted travellers, Lucius, Thrasyllus, Catullus, shining pink through diaphanous, mother-o’-pearl gleams and a slowly-lifting silvery mist, like a city of magic and fairytale. A long, long row of white palaces, with irregular gables, loomed through the mist and the gleam.

On the left, on the rocks of Lochias, the pillars of the former royal palace shone magical and fairylike in the silvery mist. Thrasyllus knew that, since Egypt had become a Roman province, the legate resided there, surrounded with royal honours. Under the palace the little square basin of the palace-harbour showed, gay with the purple sails of the legate’s triremes; with the little island of Antirrhodos, behind which pillars and yet more pillars outlined more and more clearly the white theatre; with the bight of the Posidium, where stood the Temple of Poseidon and the great Emporium, the great market of the merchant shippers, while a pier ran into the harbour on which, dainty as a marble ornament, stood the villa of the Timoneum, built by Mark Antony. A riot of luxuriant green gardens, with the stately crowns of palm-trees and the dreamy-delicate crests of tamarisks, flung cool, dark nosegays between all those gleaming white buildings, which began to blink in the ever fiercer sunshine. …

Thrasyllus pointed with his finger, along the harbour, the long row of palaces of the Caesareum, the huge docks and yards teeming all motley with people and industry, to the Heptastadium, the promenade-jetty which stretched out to unite the city with the island of Pharos, whence the lighthouse took its name. On the one side of this pier, with rostra and statues on the marble balustrade and gates, lay the harbour of Eunostus and the naval docks of Cibotus.

The water on every side was crowded and swarming with vessels: biremes and triremes, battleships and merchant-ships; the masts rose like a forest of poplars and the sails glowed like the many-coloured wings of one bird against another; and, as soon as the quadrireme glided in, she was surrounded by a host of sloops, filled with traders, with yelling Arabs and Nubians. The *Aphrodite* heaved to; a pilot came on board; then she glided on again through the press of the sloops, the yelling of the traders and with swanlike elegance turned and lay to beside the great quay, at the place where she was expected, the place kept open for her.

The quay, between the obelisks, was alive with a maddening concourse of people: sailors and merchants, vendors of fruit and water and vegetables, chattering women, screaming children, Ethiopian beggars, Greek students, priests of Serapis and Isis, Roman soldiers; and all pointed at the ship and streamed in unison to gaze at her in wide-eyed and open-mouthed admiration. For, though numbers of vessels entered the Great Harbour of Alexandria daily, it was not every day that the quay was visited by so impressive a quadrireme as this; and the beautiful ship aroused curiosity.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，茶玫瑰色黎明那柔和匀净的光，铺展在一幅奇景之上——美得像一场奇幻的梦，飘渺如幻象，摄人如魔法。大船已经驶过那座巍峨的九层大理石法罗斯，滑入大港；亚历山大里亚铺展在欣喜的旅人卢基乌斯、特拉叙卢斯、卡图卢斯眼前，透过半透明的珠母辉光与缓缓升腾的银雾，闪着粉红色，像一座魔法与童话之城。长长一列白色宫殿，山墙错落，在雾气与辉光中隐隐显现。

左边，在洛基亚斯（Lochias）的礁石上，旧日王宫的廊柱在银雾中闪着魔幻的童话般的光。特拉叙卢斯知道，自埃及成为罗马行省以来，代行官便驻跸在那里，享有帝王般的尊荣。王宫下方露出宫港那方方小小的一片港池，喜气洋洋地漂着代行官三列桨战船的紫帆；还有安提罗多斯（Antirrhodos）小岛，岛后一根根廊柱、又一根根廊柱，把白色剧院的轮廓勾得越来越清晰；还有波西迪乌姆（Posidium）湾，湾上立着波塞冬（Poseidon）神庙和宏大的恩波里乌姆——船商们的大市场；一道长堤伸进港中，堤上立着提莫尼翁（Timoneum）别墅，精致得像一件大理石饰物，是马克·安东尼（Mark Antony）所建。一片繁茂绿园的盛会，高高的棕榈树冠、柽柳梦一般纤细的梢头，把一束束清凉幽暗的花球掷进那些闪光的白色建筑之间；楼群在越来越烈的阳光下开始眨眼。……

特拉叙卢斯用手指沿港指去：长长一排恺撒庙（Caesareum）的宫殿，人畜杂沓、百业繁忙的巨大船坞与工场，一直指到赫普塔斯塔迪翁（Heptastadium）——那条伸出去把城市与法罗斯岛连成一体的长堤步道，灯塔便由岛得名。长堤一侧，大理石栏杆与大门上装点着船首饰与雕像，是欧诺斯图斯（Eunostus）港与基博托斯（Cibotus）的海军船坞。

四面水上舟船拥挤熙攘：双列桨船、三列桨船，战船与商船；桅杆密密矗立，像一片杨树林，船帆灼灼生辉，像群鸟彼此映衬的彩翼；四列桨船一滑进来，立刻被一大群小艇团团围住，艇上满是商贩，满是叫喊的阿拉伯人和努比亚人。「阿佛洛狄忒」号收桨停船；引水员登船；随后她又在小艇的重围与商贩的叫喊中滑行前进，以天鹅般的优雅转过身来，停靠在大码头边她被等候的地方——为她留出的泊位。

方尖碑之间的码头上，人头攒动，热闹得叫人发昏：水手与商人，卖水果、卖水、卖菜的小贩，喋喋不休的女人，尖声喊叫的孩子，埃塞俄比亚乞丐，希腊学生，萨拉皮斯与伊西斯的祭司，罗马士兵；人人指着大船，人潮齐齐涌来，瞪圆了眼、张大了嘴，赞叹地打量她。因为，虽说天天有船只进亚历山大里亚大港，这样气派的四列桨船光临码头却非日日都有；这条美丽的船激起了众人的好奇。

===Original===
The three travellers stood on the prow, beside the silver figure of Aphrodite, and Catullus said, in an appreciative tone:

“It’s not half bad. Just look at that row of palaces! It is as though Alexandria were one great palace, opening on its harbour! And what people, white, dark and black, all mixed! And what a noise they make, what a noise! We are much calmer in Italy! … Do look, Lucius, at all those ibises walking about on the quay, quiet and tame, pecking here and there, upon my word as though they were at home! Do you see the ibises, Thrasyllus? I thought that they just stood and dreamed on one leg, beside the Nile, like poetic birds … and, the moment I arrive, I see great flocks of them actually walking on the quay of Alexandria’s harbour! White ibises, black ibises, piebald ibises! What a crowd of ibises! What a crowd of them! And so dignified, much more dignified than the people! Ye gods, what a noisy crew the Alexandrians are!”

The gangway was slung from the ship to the quay; and the master was receiving the port-authorities, to whom he had to show his papers, when two men came hurrying across the gangway, which was hedged in by a guard of sailors to protect it against any intrusion of the gaping populace. One of them was an obvious Latin, the other a dark-skinned Sabaean.

“Well, Vettius!” said Lucius, welcoming the Latin, who was his steward. “I am glad to see you again and I hope that your voyage was as prosperous as ours!”

Vettius the steward bowed low before his young master, bowed ceremoniously before fat Uncle Catullus. He had travelled ahead of his master to seek suitable lodgings at Alexandria and he seemed very well pleased with what he had found, for he pointed joyfully to the dark-skinned Sabaean, who had kept behind and now bobbed down with many salaams and respectful assurances, uttered in a language that fluctuated between Latin, Greek, Phoenician and Arabic.

===Chinese===
三位旅人站在船首，挨着银铸的阿佛洛狄忒像，卡图卢斯用内行的口吻说：

“真不赖。瞧那一排宫殿！亚历山大里亚简直像一座向着自己的港口敞开的大宫殿！还有这些人，白的、棕的、黑的，全混在一处！他们多吵啊，多吵！咱们意大利可安静多了！……瞧啊，卢基乌斯，码头上那些朱鹭，走来走去，安安静静、不怕人，这儿啄啄那儿啄啄，说真的就跟在自己家里一样！你看见朱鹭了吗，特拉叙卢斯？我还当它们只会单腿立在尼罗河边做梦，跟诗里的鸟似的……谁知我一到，就瞧见好大一群当真在亚历山大里亚港的码头上溜达！白朱鹭、黑朱鹭、花斑朱鹭！好一群朱鹭！好大一群！还这么有派头，比人有派头多了！众神啊，亚历山大里亚人可真是一窝吵嚷的东西！”

跳板从船上搭到码头；船长正接待港务官员，向他们验看文书；这时两个人匆匆跨过跳板走来——水手们列队两旁护着跳板，不让张嘴围观的闲人靠近。一个一看便知是拉丁人，另一个是深色皮肤的萨巴人（Sabaean）。

“喂，维提乌斯（Vettius）！”卢基乌斯招呼那个拉丁人道，他是他的管家。“很高兴又见到你，但愿你的航程跟我们的一样顺遂！”

管家维提乌斯在年轻的主人面前深深一躬，又在胖胖的卡图卢斯叔父面前郑重一礼。他先行一步，替主人在亚历山大里亚觅取合适的住处，如今看来对所获颇为得意，因为他喜滋滋地指着那个深色皮肤的萨巴人——那人一直缀在后面，这时连连打躬作揖，一叠声地恭敬保证，说的是一种在拉丁语、希腊语、腓尼基语和阿拉伯语之间摇摆不定的语言。

===Original===
“This is Master Ghizla, a native of Saba, my lord,” said Vettius, presenting him, “the owner of the largest guesthouse in Alexandria; and he has at your disposal a row of three suites, standing in their own gardens, with spacious annexes, near the guesthouse proper; and I am convinced that, when we have put in our own furniture, they will afford a fit residence for you and the honourable Catullus. Of course, when travelling, all conveniences are but temporary and not to be compared with your insula at Rome or your villa at Baiae, now the property of our gracious Emperor Tiberius.”

“It is well, it is well, Vettius,” said Lucius. “We shall not be too hard to please. Are there baths attached to them?”

“There are most comfortable baths attached to them, my lord,” declared Master Ghizla, bobbing down twice and thrice in salaams. “And there are taps with very cold water and taps with very hot water. They are suites which I let only to princely nobles like your lordship; and I have had the honour of lodging in them the Persian Prince Kardusi, whom you surely know, and Baabab, Satrap of Mesopotamia, whom you also surely know, my lords, as princely nobles.”

“Certainly, certainly,” replied Lucius, trying to jest. “Kardusi and Baabab, I know them well.”

“We are even related to them and call them by their names,” Uncle Catullus broke in, airily, with a bow and puffing out his stomach. “But there is something that *I* want to ask you, Master Ghizla, something that neither his lordship nor Master Vettius will care so much about: are there *kitchens* to the suites, kitchens where our trusty cook can prepare us this or that simple fare?”

“There are most comfortable kitchens to these princely suites, my lord,” Master Ghizla assured him. “His Highness the Satrap Baabab often gave very sumptuous banquets and would invite his excellency the legate to his table every other day; and near the kitchens there is a well of water clear as crystal.”

“I don’t drink much water,” said Uncle Catullus.

“We have old Mareotis wine in our cellars, my lord, wine thick as ink, dark-purple as molten princely sealing-wax and fragrant as the own lotus of our Lady Isis, blessed be her name! We have also the rose-coloured date-wine of Meroe and the fine topaz-yellow liqueur of Napata: we have all the Ethiopian liqueurs. …”

“That’s better than water,” said Uncle Catullus, smacking his lips. “What say you, my dear Lucius?”

===Chinese===
“这位是吉兹拉老板（Master Ghizla），萨巴本地人，老爷，”维提乌斯引见道，“亚历山大里亚最大客栈的主人；他手边有一排三进的套院，各自带着独立的花园，还有宽敞的附属屋舍，就在客栈近旁；我深信，等我们把自家的家具陈设布置进去，足够您和尊贵的卡图卢斯体面安住。当然，出门在外，一切便利都是将就，比不得您罗马的宅邸，也比不得巴伊亚的别墅——如今那可是我们仁慈的皇帝提比略的产业了。”

“好，好，维提乌斯，”卢基乌斯说。“我们不至于太难伺候。套院带不带浴间？”

“带着极舒适的浴间，老爷，”吉兹拉老板说着，接连打了两三个躬。“还有出极冷的水的龙头，也有出极热的水的龙头。这样的套院，我只租给您老爷这样的王公贵人；我有幸在里头招待过波斯王子卡尔杜西（Kardusi）——您想必认识——还有美索不达米亚总督巴阿巴布（Baabab）——诸位老爷想必也认识——都是王公贵人。”

“当然，当然，”卢基乌斯想打趣似的答道。“卡尔杜西和巴阿巴布，我熟得很。”

“我们还是他们的亲戚呢，连名字都跟着他们叫，”卡图卢斯叔父大大咧咧地插进来，一躬身，鼓起肚子。“不过有件事得*我*来问你，吉兹拉老板，一件老爷和维提乌斯先生都不怎么放在心上的事：这些套院带不带*厨房*？有没有厨房，好让我们那位可靠的厨子给我们做点这样那样的家常吃食？”

“这些王公套院带着极舒服的厨房，老爷，”吉兹拉老板打包票。“巴阿巴布总督大人殿下常在那儿设豪宴，隔天便请代行官大人赴席；厨房旁边还有一口井，水清得像水晶。”

“我不大喝水，”卡图卢斯叔父说。

“我们的酒窖里有马雷奥蒂斯陈酒，老爷，那酒稠得像墨，紫得像化开的王公火漆，香得像我们的伊西斯夫人本人的莲花——愿她的圣名受赞美！我们还有麦罗埃玫瑰色的椰枣酒，还有纳帕塔上好的黄玉色利口酒：埃塞俄比亚的利口酒我们应有尽有。……”

“这可比水强，”卡图卢斯叔父咂着嘴说。“你说呢，亲爱的卢基乌斯？”

===Original===
Lucius had made a great effort that morning to control his grief; together with his uncle and the tutor, he had stared with interest at the splendid panorama that unrolled itself before their eyes as they entered the Great Harbour; he had welcomed his steward Vettius with a kind word; he had interested himself in the apartments which he was to occupy. Now, however, tired and listless, he had sunk into a seat, beside the silver image of the goddess, and sat looking disconsolately in front of him. He was a tall, comely fellow, with an athletic frame developed by wrestling exercise; and his dark eyes, though now veiled with melancholy and longing, gleamed with a deep spark of intelligence. Immensely rich, the sole heir of various relatives who had died childless, he had joined for but a short time in the mad orgies of the young Romans of his own rank and had soon devoted himself to many branches of science, to astronomy in particular, philosophy, magic, the favourite passion of that period; he amused himself with modelling and sculpture; as a collector, he loved everything that was beautiful: pictures and statues, old coins and old glass; and his Etruscan antiquities were famous all over Rome. Certainly, he had always desired to see Egypt, to travel through Egypt; and the sight of the marble palaces of Alexandria had already charmed him for a moment. But his grief and longing returned to him immediately after; red anger awoke in him once more and impotent fury that Ilia, his best-beloved slave, had vanished, one inauspicious morning, from his villa at Baiae, without leaving a trace behind her.

“Come, Lucius,” said Catullus, “we’re going on shore now, my dear fellow. There are our litters waiting for us, prepared by Master Ghizla’s care. …”

“With excellent, powerful Libyan bearers, my lord, bearers whom I reserve exclusively for princely nobles like you. …”

“And, if you care first to take a turn through the city, sir,” Vettius proffered, “I will see to it that the furniture and baggage are conveyed from the ships to your apartments, so that you will find everything arranged in time for luncheon.”

Although Lucius of course travelled with his own litters and his own bearers, Ghizla and Vettius had judged that two Alexandrian litters, with twelve Libyan bearers, would serve his purpose better at Alexandria, especially because here they were accustomed to move quicker, at a trot, than in Rome, where the pace was statelier and slower. Master Ghizla, therefore, who would not fail to charge the litters and bearers in his bill at double the price and more, had quickly and slyly set out his litters in front of the gangway, before Rufus, the under-steward, had even thought of preparing his master’s own litter.

===Chinese===
那天早上，卢基乌斯为克制悲伤下了大力气；他同叔父和教师一起，饶有兴致地注视着驶入大港时在眼前展开的壮丽全景；他亲切地招呼了管家维提乌斯；他对将入住的套院也颇为上心。可此刻，他倦怠无力，颓然坐倒在女神银像旁边的座位里，怏怏地望着前方。他是个身材颀长、面目俊美的男子，摔跤锻炼出一副运动员的筋骨；一双深色的眼睛虽然此刻蒙着忧郁与渴望，却仍闪着深沉的智慧火花。他豪富无比，是几位身后无嗣的亲属的唯一继承人；同阶层的罗马青年的疯狂荒唐事他只沾过一小阵，很快便潜心于多门学问，尤好天文，还有哲学与法术——那是当世的时髦癖好；他玩票似的弄弄塑造与雕刻；作为收藏家，凡美的东西他无不喜爱：绘画与雕像，古钱与古玻璃；他的伊特鲁里亚古物名满罗马。当然，他一直渴望看看埃及，游历埃及；亚历山大里亚的大理石宫殿也已让他陶醉了片刻。但悲伤与渴望随即又回到他心里；红色的怒火再度燃起，还有那无能为力的狂怒——伊利娅，他最心爱的女奴，在一个不祥的早晨，从他巴伊亚的别墅里消失得无影无踪。

“来吧，卢基乌斯，”卡图卢斯说，“咱们这就上岸，亲爱的。我们的肩舆在那边候着呢，是吉兹拉老板费心备下的。……”

“配着出色、强壮的利比亚轿夫，老爷，这样的轿夫我只留给您这样的王公贵人专享。……”

“还有，老爷，您若想先在城里兜上一圈，”维提乌斯进言道，“我来安排把家具行李从船上运到您的套院，保管您回来吃午饭时一切都布置停当。”

虽说卢基乌斯出门自然带着自家的肩舆和轿夫，吉兹拉和维提乌斯却都认定，两乘亚历山大里亚式的肩舆、十二名利比亚轿夫，在本地更能派上用场，尤其因为此地的肩舆惯于小跑前行，比罗马那种庄重缓慢的步子快得多。于是吉兹拉老板——他绝不会忘记在账单上把肩舆和轿夫按双倍乃至更高的价钱开列——已经抢在副管家鲁弗斯（Rufus）还没想起备置主人自家肩舆之前，狡黠地把他的肩舆飞快摆到了跳板前面。

===Original===
“Very well, Vettius,” said Lucius, making an effort and rising. “I see two litters: those are for Uncle Catullus and me. And how is our good Thrasyllus to accompany us? For he knows the city already from the writings of Eratosthenes and Strabo; he can tell us much that is interesting on the way; and the tour would not afford us half the same pleasure without him.”

“I have had a good donkey saddled for Master Thrasyllus,” said Master Ghizla, with a salaam.

In fact, an ass, held by a boy on a leading-rein, stood waiting behind the litters, among the open-mouthed populace.

“And if,” the Sabaean hinted, suavely, “if I might entrust the noble lords to the conduct of my younger brother Caleb, he will go in front of the noble lords and act as a guide with whom they will doubtless be no less satisfied than were the Prince of Persia and the Satrap of Mesopotamia. …”

===Chinese===
“很好，维提乌斯，”卢基乌斯振作精神，站起身来。“我看见两乘肩舆：是给卡图卢斯叔父和我的。那我们的好特拉叙卢斯怎么随行呢？他可是从埃拉托色尼（Eratosthenes）和斯特拉波（Strabo）的著作里早就认得这座城了；一路上他能给我们讲许多有趣的东西；没有他，这趟游览的乐趣可要减去一半。”

“我已经给特拉叙卢斯先生备了一头好驴，”吉兹拉老板一躬身说。

果然，一头驴子由一个男孩牵着缰绳，候在肩舆后面，夹在张嘴围观的百姓中间。

“还有，倘若，”萨巴人温文尔雅地暗示道，“倘若我可以把各位贵人老爷托付给我弟弟卡莱布（Caleb）引路，他会走在贵人老爷们前面充当向导，两位老爷必定满意，绝不亚于波斯王子和美索不达米亚总督。……”

===Original===
“Kardusi and Baabab,” Uncle Catullus completed, mischievously. “Two pleasant, simple fellows: I’m sorry they’re gone.”

But Ghizla pointed to Caleb, who now came up with a flourish of salaams and bowed. As against Ghizla, who was tall, lean and dignified, Caleb, the younger man, was vivacious and sparkling, with dark eyes, flashing teeth and a gay, smiling mouth. He wore wide striped trousers of many colours, a white burnous, a red turban and large rings in his ears; and he spoke better Latin than his brother, with now and then a few sentences of Greek.

Lucius accepted Caleb as his guide; and they went on shore; and Lucius and Uncle Catullus took their seats in their litters. Thrasyllus mounted his quiet donkey; but Caleb flung himself with a swagger on to a jet-black, gaily-caparisoned Sabaean mare, who neighed when she felt the red heels of Caleb’s sandals in her flanks. So the procession started: first three ebon-black outrunners, with whips which they cracked right and left to make room, to drive barking dogs away and to keep beggars at a distance; then Caleb, proudly sitting his horse like a young conqueror, always smiling and sparkling with black eyes and white teeth; then the two litters, with Thrasyllus at the side on his donkey; and round the three travellers a number of guards, armed with whips and sticks.

===Chinese===
“卡尔杜西和巴阿巴布，”卡图卢斯叔父淘气地替他说完。“两个和气的老实人：他们走了真可惜。”

吉兹拉指了指卡莱布，卡莱布这时飘着一串躬身礼走上前来。与高瘦端庄的吉兹拉相反，弟弟卡莱布年轻活泼、神采飞扬，深色的眼睛、雪亮的牙齿、一张爱笑的嘴。他穿着宽大的彩色条纹裤、白色布努斯（burnous，阿拉伯斗篷）、红色头巾，耳朵上戴着大耳环；他的拉丁语比哥哥说得好，还不时夹上几句希腊语。

卢基乌斯接受了卡莱布做向导；一行人上了岸；卢基乌斯和卡图卢斯叔父坐进肩舆。特拉叙卢斯跨上他那头安分的驴子；卡莱布却神气活现地一跃，跨上一匹乌黑油亮、鞍辔华丽的萨巴母马，卡莱布红凉鞋的鞋跟一磕马胁，母马便嘶鸣起来。于是队伍出发了：最前面是三名乌木般黑的开道仆役，挥着鞭子左右开弓辟出路来，赶开狂吠的狗，隔开乞丐；随后是卡莱布，像个年轻的征服者昂然骑在马上，黑眼睛白牙齿一直笑着闪着；接着是两乘肩舆，特拉叙卢斯骑着驴傍在一侧；三位旅人周围还围着一队卫兵，携着鞭子短棍。

===Original===
They pressed through the crowd along the quay, where everybody looked and pointed at the distinguished foreigners; they went at a quick trot, for the outrunners went at a trot, cracking their whips; Caleb, on his Sabaean mare, showed off his equestrian powers and pranced elegantly along upon his steed; the litter-bearers followed at a short, steady quick-trot; even Thrasyllus’ donkey, as sober as a philosopher, trotted blithely on; and behind trotted the guards, shaking their sticks and flourishing their long whips. They trotted along the middle of the broad street, over the great stone flags; and it seemed as though everything were trotting in a quick rhythm, including all the other litters, the carts and horsemen, who with their outrunners and outriders also strove to make their way through the bustle.

So the cavalcade trotted on; and the street-boys scattered and the ibises scattered with outstretched necks and wide-flapping wings.

“What crowds of ibises!” Catullus cried. “Thrasyllus, isn’t it comical to see so many ibises walking and fluttering through the streets of Alexandria?”

“My lord,” cried Thrasyllus, from the back of his dancing donkey, “the ibises are Alexandria’s scavengers.”

“I dare say, but they are unclean birds themselves for all that! And they are counted among the sacred animals!” cried Uncle Catullus. “Whoosh! Whoosh!”

===Chinese===
他们在人群里沿着码头挤过去，人人都在张望指点这几位显赫的外邦人；队伍小跑前行，因为开道仆役一路小跑，鞭子甩得噼啪作响；卡莱布骑着萨巴母马卖弄骑术，胯下骏马优雅地踏着快步；轿夫们迈着短促而平稳的快步紧随；连特拉叙卢斯那头驴子，哲学家般持重，也欢快地小跑起来；后面是卫兵，颠着短棍、舞着长鞭小跑跟随。一行人沿着宽阔大街的中央、踏着巨大的石板小跑前行；仿佛万物都踏着急促的节奏小跑，包括别的肩舆、大车和骑手——他们也各自带着开道仆役和随行马夫，在喧嚣中奋力开路。

马队就这样小跑着行进；街上的顽童四散躲开，朱鹭也伸长脖颈、扑扇着翅膀四散躲开。

“好多的朱鹭！”卡图卢斯喊道。“特拉叙卢斯，看到这么多朱鹭在亚历山大里亚的街上走来走去、扑扑棱棱，是不是很滑稽？”

“老爷，”特拉叙卢斯从他那头颠跳的驴背上喊道，“朱鹭是亚历山大里亚的清道夫。”

“就算是这样吧，可它们自己也一样是脏鸟！它们还位列圣兽呢！”卡图卢斯叔父喊道。“嘘！嘘！”

===Original===
And he drove them away, with a flourish of his arm from the litter, for the whips of the runners trotting behind circled, it is true, around the street-boys but ever spared the sacred ibises, one of which would sometimes stray, fluttering wildly, among the bearers.

Meanwhile Caleb continued to give a graceful equestrian performance on his snorting mare beside Lucius’ litter:

“My lord!” cried Caleb. “Do you see the Heptastadium? The great bridge leading to the Pharos? Do you see that tall-masted ships are able to sail under it? It is an interesting walk there of an evening, my lord: all the beauties of Alexandria go there; and a great nobleman like yourself need but make his choice and any hetaira in Alexandria will fall at his feet! This is the Moon Gate, my lord! And this is the High Street: behind it lies the Rhacotis quarter, which is very interesting at night, my lord, most interesting for anyone like your lordship to roam through in disguise. But now we are going through the High Street; and here, you see, is the Square, where the High Street crosses the Museum Street and the Avenue of Pillars.”

Lucius looked around him with enjoyment. They were still going at a trot, a trot of mare and runners and bearers and donkey, a noisy trotting between shouting and laughing voices and cracking whips, while in the streets and squares the hucksters also shouted and laughed and swore, while the street-boys cheered and screamed for an obolus and the ibises, flapping their wings, darted away, to alight again elsewhere and act as scavengers to Alexandria.

“It is very different from Rome in every way,” thought Lucius. “It is the east.”

===Chinese===
他从肩舆里挥臂驱赶朱鹭，因为后面小跑的开道仆役的鞭子虽然绕着顽童打转，却总是放过神圣的朱鹭——其中偶尔有一只乱扑着翅膀迷了路，钻进轿夫中间。

与此同时，卡莱布在卢基乌斯肩舆旁那匹打着响鼻的母马上，继续表演他优雅的骑术：

“老爷！”卡莱布喊道。“您看见赫普塔斯塔迪翁了吗？通往法罗斯的那座大桥？您看见高桅的船都能从桥下驶过吗？傍晚在那儿散步很有意思，老爷：亚历山大里亚的美人全去那儿；像您这样的大贵人只消挑一挑，亚历山大里亚随便哪位名妓（hetaira）都会拜倒在您脚下！这是月神门，老爷！这是大街：大街后面是拉科提斯（Rhacotis）区，夜里非常有意思，老爷，像您老爷这样的人化装夜游，再有趣不过。不过现在我们走大街；瞧，这儿是广场，大街在这儿与博学园街和柱廊大道相交。”

卢基乌斯饶有兴味地环顾四周。队伍仍在小跑，母马、开道仆役、轿夫和驴子的小跑，在叫喊、欢笑与鞭哨之间的喧闹小跑；街市里的小贩也在叫喊、欢笑、咒骂，顽童们为讨一个奥波勒斯欢呼尖叫，朱鹭扑着翅膀飞窜开去，落到别处，继续做亚历山大里亚的清道夫。

“这跟罗马样样都不同，”卢基乌斯想。“这是东方。”

===Original===
Yes, it was the east. It was Egypt, it was Alexandria. Never in the Forum at Rome, lively and busy though it was, never in the basilicas had Lucius beheld this ever trotting, ever hurrying tumult. It was as though everyone were pressed for time and hurrying feverishly. Processions of priests hurried; the Roman guards even, returning from the Palace after being relieved, marched with an accelerated step; and yet the numerous litters never struck against one another: they all glided at the trot of their bearers, to the right, to the left, beside one another; there was only a shouting, a din, a cursing, a cracking of whips loud enough to rouse the dead. Here was a quarrel, with violent gestures and shrill voices; there the noisy gaiety of squabbling vegetable-women and bawling vendors of watermelons; suddenly, in a rage, the women flung cabbages at the vendors’ heads and the vendors sent melons trundling between the women’s legs; the cabbages and melons rolled across the street and the crowd yelled with enjoyment, while distinguished but still trotting processions of notables in litters or on horseback made a way for themselves. The cabbages and melons rolled in front of the feet of Lucius’ bearers; and Caleb, rising in his stirrups with flapping burnous and uplifted arms, hardly holding the reins in his fingers while the mare reared on her hind-legs, poured forth a torrent of curses over the women and the hucksters … and then turned to Lucius with a pleased smile, as though all this tumult were the most ordinary morning affair in the streets of Alexandria. … Yes, that was the Egyptian character: bustle, tumult, uproar, yelling and cursing for the least thing; quarrelling for the least thing; and then everything just ordinary again, as though nothing had happened. All this in a motley whirl of colours: Rome was monotonously white and colourless beside it, Lucius thought. Here the colours glared more fiercely: the citrons, oranges and melons lay yellow and gold over the markets; and there were exotic fruits too, scarlet and vermilion. …

They came to the painters’ quarter. Troughs of used colouring-matter ran in gutters along the streets: there were rivulets of indigo, there were little waterfalls of ochre. The bearers splashed through purple and trotted on with purple-black feet. A golden whirl of dust in the morning sun powdered over these motley colours as with handfuls of the finest glittering sand. Tall buildings shot up their pillars in that glitter, seemed to shimmer, to move in that shimmer of light.

Caleb now pointed to the Acropolis, standing fortress-like, foursquare and heavy, protecting and dominating the city. Next came the Sun Gate. Outside the city-wall was a canal; along the canal ran an avenue of tall sycamores, bringing a sudden blissful calm and coolness and silver-green shadows. And now Caleb pointed to the famous lake, Lake Mareotis: it lay spread out like a sea, but was divided by isthmuses into smaller inland lakes; there were islands often bearing some temple to Aphrodite; and along the margins of the lake rose villa after villa, in royal pomp of marble-coloured villas, casting their reflections into the limpid water.

===Chinese===
是的，这是东方。这是埃及，这是亚历山大里亚。罗马的广场，无论多么热闹繁忙，还有那些巴西利卡会堂，都从不曾让卢基乌斯见过这种永远小跑、永远匆忙的喧腾。仿佛人人都赶时间，都在发热似的奔忙。祭司的队列匆匆而行；连换岗归来离开王宫的罗马卫兵，也迈着加急的步伐；然而众多的肩舆却从不相互碰撞：它们全都随着轿夫的碎步滑行，向右，向左，彼此并行；只有喊叫、喧嚣、咒骂，鞭声脆得能把死人吵醒。这边一场争吵，指手画脚，尖声叫嚷；那边是吵吵嚷嚷取乐的卖菜妇人和扯着嗓子叫卖西瓜的小贩；女人们突然火起，把卷心菜扔到小贩头上，小贩便把甜瓜滚进女人们腿间；卷心菜和甜瓜滚过街面，人群看得大声叫好，而那些乘着肩舆、骑着马、显贵却依旧小跑的名人队伍，自顾自地从中开出路来。卷心菜和甜瓜滚到卢基乌斯轿夫们的脚前；卡莱布在马镫里站起身，布努斯翻飞，双臂高举，手指间松松挽着缰绳，母马用后腿人立起来，他对着那些女人和小贩倾泻出一串咒骂……随后转向卢基乌斯，一脸得意的笑，仿佛这场喧嚣不过是亚历山大里亚街头一件顶平常的早晨小事。……是的，这就是埃及人的性子：为一点小事奔忙、骚动、吵嚷、叫骂；为一点小事争吵；然后一切照旧，仿佛什么也没发生过。这一切裹在斑斓的色彩漩涡里：相形之下，罗马是单调的白、是无色，卢基乌斯想。这里的色彩更烈：香橼、橙子和甜瓜黄黄金金地堆满集市；还有异域的水果，猩红与朱红。……

他们来到颜料匠区。用剩的颜料在街旁的明沟里流淌：有靛蓝的小溪，有赭石的小瀑布。轿夫们溅着紫水蹚过，脚染成紫黑色继续小跑。朝阳里一团金色的尘雾，如撒下的一把把最细的亮沙，罩在这些斑斓的颜色上。高楼把廊柱抛进那片闪光里，似乎在颤动，在光的闪烁中浮动。

卡莱布这时指向卫城：它堡垒般矗立着，方方正正、沉重厚实，护佑并俯瞰着全城。接着是太阳门。城墙外是一条运河；沿河是一道高大的西克莫无花果树林荫道，蓦然带来一片清凉的安谧和银绿色的浓荫。这时卡莱布又指向那座著名的湖——马雷奥蒂斯湖：它铺展开来像一片海，却被地峡分隔成几处较小的内湖；湖中岛屿往往立着某座阿佛洛狄忒的神庙；沿湖岸边，一幢别墅接着一幢别墅耸起，带着大理石别墅的帝王气派，把倒影投进清澈的水里。

===Original===
“That is where the rich hetairae live,” said Caleb, with a wink, “hetairae for people like your lordship: a prince like you can take your choice.”

Tall papyrus shot up on the lake’s edge. There were papyrus-eyots: the stalks rustled at the least breeze; and on the eyots lived the basket-makers: there were families of basket-makers; the children weaving baskets and hampers looked up and cried out for an obolus. White lotus and pink water-lilies blossomed and small gilt barges passed across the lake, with coloured awnings to them. Ibises and cranes fluttered out of the reeds.

“You must come here again in the evening, my lord,” Caleb advised, winking eagerly. “This is the place for one like your lordship to enjoy himself: at Rhacotis there are only the common women and the houses where the sailors go. But many princely nobles like to see everything at Alexandria.”

The procession trotted back through the Gate of the Sun, which made a wide breach in the city-walls, a vaulted arch above Corinthian pillars; and Caleb said:

“We are now coming to the Avenue of Pillars and to the Museum.”

===Chinese===
“阔气的名妓就住在那儿，”卡莱布挤挤眼说，“伺候您老爷这样人的名妓：像您这样的王子只消挑就是。”

高高的纸莎草在湖边拔起。有一座座纸莎草小岛：微风一吹苇秆便沙沙作响；岛上住着编篮人家：一户一户的编篮人；编着篮子篓子的孩子们抬起头，喊着讨一个奥波勒斯。白莲与粉色睡莲开着花，一条条镀金的小艇张着彩篷渡过湖面。朱鹭和鹤从芦苇丛里扑棱棱飞起。

“您晚上一定得再来这儿，老爷，”卡莱布急切地挤着眼劝道。“像您老爷这样的人寻乐子就该在这儿：拉科提斯只有下等女人和水手们去的窑子。不过许多王公贵人也喜欢把亚历山大里亚什么都看个遍。”

队伍小跑着从太阳门折返——那门在城墙上豁开一道宽阔的缺口，科林斯式廊柱上架着券拱；卡莱布说：

“我们现在就去柱廊大道和博学园。”

===Original===
Here again were the bustle, the tumult, the uproar, the shouting and cheering and cursing, the multitude, litters, horsemen, pedestrians. The Avenue of Pillars also was swarming, mainly with students, philosophers and loose women. The sun blazed down in the middle of the street; there were golden patches of light and blue-purple islands of shadow. And there was always the golden glitter of dust, as of the finest sand whirling through the air. Here were the hairdressers and barbers; here were the baths, here the tailors’-shops with their riot of colours and here the glittering jewellers’-shops and here, behind tables, stood the money-changers. There were stretches of green garden; and behind the gardens loomed the colonnades of the Museum. Close by were the Gymnasium and the Athletic School.

“Will your lordship visit the Museum?” asked Caleb, still parading his horsemanship on the Sabaean mare.

Thrasyllus thought that it would be interesting to visit the Museum; and the travellers alighted. There was a great rush to see them. Uncle Catullus threw oboli among the street-boys, who rolled over one another, fighting. Beggars approached, grey-bearded men like prophets and old women like sibyls; and Lucius flung a coin here and there.

===Chinese===
这里又是那份奔忙、喧腾、吵嚷，欢呼与咒骂，还是那么多人，肩舆、骑手、行人。柱廊大道也熙熙攘攘，主要是学生、哲学家和荡妇。太阳当街直射下来；地上是一块块金色的光斑、一座座蓝紫色的影子岛。永远有金色的尘土在闪光，像最细的沙在空气里回旋。这里是理发师和剃须匠；这里是浴场，这里是色彩喧闹的裁缝铺，这里是珠光宝气的珠宝店；这里，桌子后面站着钱币兑换商。有一段段绿色园圃；园圃后面耸起博学园（the Museum）的柱廊。近旁是体育馆和竞技学校。

“老爷要参观博学园吗？”卡莱布问，还在那匹萨巴母马上炫耀他的骑术。

特拉叙卢斯觉得参观博学园会很有意思；旅人们下了肩舆。众人蜂拥来看他们。卡图卢斯叔父把奥波勒斯掷给街上的顽童，孩子们滚作一团，打起架来。乞丐们凑上前来，是些胡须灰白、像先知的男人和像女巫的老妇；卢基乌斯这儿那儿抛着钱币。

===Original===
The runners and guards drew themselves up around the two litters, the mare and the donkey; but Caleb walked in front of the travellers, mincing elegantly on the tips of his red riding-boots and holding the hem of his burnous in his swaying hand. It was as though he were always dancing, whether on horseback or on foot.

“The Museum,” Caleb explained, “is, as your lordships know, the Academy of Alexandria, founded by the beautiful Cleopatra.”

“That is not true,” Thrasyllus whispered to his young master. “It was founded by Ptolemy the First.”

“Here philosophers and scholars in every branch of science devote themselves to study; and they are surrounded by thousands of disciples from all countries. But both masters and pupils are as poor as rats and do not, all told, possess … *that!*” said Caleb, with a flip of his finger and thumb.

“The Museum has produced great scholars,” Thrasyllus expounded, more appreciatively, “such as Euclid, Erasistratus and Diophantus; then there were the poets Theocritus, Aratus, Callimachus; and among critics Aristarchus; and among philosophers more than I could name.”

===Chinese===
开道仆役和卫兵在两乘肩舆、母马和驴子周围列队肃立；卡莱布走在旅人们前面，红色马靴的靴尖优雅地迈着碎步，一只摇曳的手提着布努斯的下摆。他仿佛总在跳舞，不论骑在马上还是走在地上。

“博学园，”卡莱布讲解道，“诸位老爷知道，是亚历山大里亚的学宫，美丽的克娄巴特拉（Cleopatra）创办的。”

“这不是真的，”特拉叙卢斯对年轻的主人耳语道。“是托勒密一世（Ptolemy the First）创办的。”

“这里的哲学家和各门学问的学者潜心治学；他们身边围着来自各国的成千弟子。可是老师带学生全都穷得像老鼠，全部家当拢共也拿不出……*这个！*”卡莱布说着，拇指食指啪地一捻。

“博学园出过大学者，”特拉叙卢斯较为赞赏地讲说道，“如欧几里得（Euclid）、埃拉西斯特拉图斯（Erasistratus）和丢番图（Diophantus）；还有诗人忒奥克里托斯（Theocritus）、阿拉托斯（Aratus）、卡利马科斯（Callimachus）；批评家中有阿里斯塔科斯（Aristarchus）；哲学家之多，我数都数不过来。”

===Original===
“And because they are so poor, all these learned gentry,” said Caleb, with a laugh, pointing, as they entered the gardens of the Museum through a portico, to stately white-cloaked figures walking to and fro, “because they are so poor, they live on a fund provided by the State: they’re no use for anything, these learned gentry; but they are certainly clever, my lords, they’re all that: you won’t find their equals for cleverness anywhere. And the books they collect! Their library is quite famous. … Look,” continued Caleb, pointing, “it is just the time when they have their midday meal: it seems to be philosophical to do so earlier than princely nobles are used to do. No doubt it will interest you, as strangers, to see so many very wise and poverty-stricken scholars and philosophers eating their black broth.”

The colonnades of the Museum loomed aloft; there were statues to commemorate famous men of learning; and there was an immense rounded exedra, from which lectures were delivered at frequent intervals. The travellers entered the cenaculum, the refectory, which was wide, lofty and very long; the scholars and philosophers sat eating at long tables; Lucius was struck by the fact that they were sitting, instead of reclining.

“They don’t know any better,” Caleb explained. “They just sit down for a moment and gobble up their broth; they are not epicures, they are only just clever, you see. They have more in their heads, my lords, than in their pockets. But they have plenty in their heads beyond a doubt.”

===Chinese===
“正因为他们这么穷，这些有学问的爷们儿，”卡莱布笑着，一面指点——他们正穿过门廊走进博学园的园林，望见一群高贵的一袭白袍、来回踱步的身影——“正因为他们这么穷，他们靠国家出的一笔经费过活：这些有学问的爷们儿干什么都不中用；可他们确实聪明，老爷们，绝顶聪明：论聪明，哪儿也找不着他们的对手。还有他们收的书！他们的图书馆大大有名。……瞧，”卡莱布继续指点道，“正赶上他们吃午饭的时辰：看来比王公贵人习惯的时辰吃得早些，也算是一种哲学。看到这么多绝顶智慧、一贫如洗的学者哲学家喝他们的黑汤，诸位远客想必觉得有趣。”

博学园的柱廊高高耸起；有纪念历代名儒的雕像；还有一个巨大的半圆讲坛，讲学频频从那里开讲。旅人们走进食堂——宽敞、高阔、极长；学者和哲学家们坐在长桌旁吃饭；卢基乌斯注意到他们是坐着吃，而不是斜倚着吃。

“他们不懂别的讲究，”卡莱布解释道。“他们只坐一小会儿，呼噜噜把汤喝完；他们不是美食家，他们只是聪明，您瞧。他们脑袋里的东西，老爷们，比口袋里的多。不过脑袋里的东西毫无疑问多得很。”

===Original===
A philosopher moved towards the strangers. He was very old, frail and grey and looked like a long dry stalk, in his toga. He smiled and mumbled words which at first were incomprehensible. From the folds of his garment he stretched forth a clawlike hand. He was begging; and Lucius gave him some money.

“The highest philosophy is … to be satisfied with little,” he then said, plainly, in pure Greek.

And he bowed, ironically, and turned away with the movement of a long dry stalk, in his dirty cloak.

“The shameless rascal!” cried Uncle Catullus, indignantly.

But Lucius laughed and looked down the long table at which the men of learning ate. Sometimes a beggar would come up to them; and they gave him their bread and fruit. Sometimes, too, dogs snuffled around; and the men of learning flung them their offal, over which the dogs choked greedily. Two ibises also walked in ludicrous high-legged state through the cenaculum, pecking here and there, and kept the floor clean, though they themselves were not so cleanly.

===Chinese===
一位哲学家向生客们挪近过来。他非常苍老、干瘦而灰白，裹着托加，像一根干枯的长茎。他微笑着，起初咕哝出一些叫人听不懂的话。他从衣褶里伸出一只爪子似的手。他在行乞；卢基乌斯给了他一些钱。

“最高的哲学便是……知足于少，”随后他用纯正的希腊语，清清楚楚地说。

他讥诮地一躬身，像一根干枯的长茎那样转开去，裹着他那件肮脏的斗篷。

“不要脸的老无赖！”卡图卢斯叔父愤慨地喊道。

卢基乌斯却笑了，顺着长桌望下去，那些有学问的人正在用餐。不时有乞丐走上前去；他们把自己的面包和果子给他。有时狗也嗅来嗅去；有学问的人把下水抛给它们，狗贪婪地噎着喉咙大嚼。还有两只朱鹭，迈着滑稽的高步、派头十足地穿过食堂，东啄西啄，把地面拾掇干净，虽然它们自己并不那么干净。

===Original===
The travellers returned to their litters; and, amid much shouting and cursing and swearing at street-boys and cracking of whips at beggars, the procession started, while Caleb, for no reason, insisted on making his mare rear and curvet across the street with elegant movements of her forefeet.

But now, smiling with his black eyes and white teeth, he bent to one side, low enough almost to slip from his mount, and asked Lucius:

“Would your lordship now like to see the Soma?”

And through the public gardens of Bruchium, along the Paneum—an artificial little rocky mountain built up in the shape of a top or pineapple—the procession trotted to the Soma, the burying-place of the Ptolemies, where it lay in the cool shade of sycamores and tamarisks. A long avenue of recumbent sphinxes, male—bearded—and female—high-breasted—led to the pyramid tombs. The travellers alighted and the old priests in charge appeared.

===Chinese===
旅人们回到肩舆上；在对顽童的连声喝骂、朝乞丐的鞭响之中，队伍重新出发；卡莱布又毫无来由地非让他的母马前蹄腾空、斜穿街心腾跃不可，前蹄的动作优雅极了。

可这会儿，他黑眼睛白牙齿笑吟吟地侧下身去，低得几乎要从鞍上滑落，问卢基乌斯：

“老爷这会儿想看看索玛（Soma）吗？”

队伍穿过布鲁基乌姆的公共园林，绕过帕涅乌姆（Paneum）——一座按陀螺或菠萝形状堆起来的假山小石山——小跑着来到索玛，托勒密诸王的葬地；它卧在西克莫无花果树与柽柳清凉的浓荫里。一条卧姿斯芬克斯像的长长甬道——雄的有须，雌的高乳——通向金字塔形的陵墓。旅人们下了肩舆，负责看管的老祭司们现身出来。

===Original===
“These distinguished strangers wish to see the burial-places of the Ptolemies,” said Caleb. “They are princely nobles and no doubt will also be interested in the tomb of Alexander the Great.”

“Death is but a slumbering and a twilight transition to the halls of eternal sunshine,” replied the priest in charge. “Earthly greatness is the perishable step to the imperishable palace of Osiris, where our dead monarchs now sit enthroned around him, their heads circled with the pschent and their hands grasping the scarab sceptre. And great Isis has appeared to them as the splendour of truth, for she lifted her veil for their delight, so that they saw her. Life is but a dream, death is a bridge and eternity is life.”

Caleb walked mincingly in front, on the tips of his red riding-boots, and pointed out things, while the old priest went on reciting the eternal verities, as though to himself, The tombs of granite, porphyry and marble, inscribed with hieroglyphics, rose like temples, pyramid-shaped. The priest now went in front of the travellers and descended a few steps: inside, in the subterranean vault, invisible, the mummies rested in their painted sarcophagi; standing lamps burned on their tripods, perfumes rose in a cloud from vases and dishes; and daintily-coloured glass vessels, filled with oil, honey and fruit, stood on low bronze tables, while amphorae of consecrated water awaited the hour of the resurrection, when the dead should rise and be baptized into the true new life, which was eternity. There was an overpowering scent of sickly-sweet aromatics; and in the mist of the perfumes the big, wide-open eyes of the painted images on the sarcophagus-lids stared, ghostly and superhuman, straight before them into the brightening future. They were images of bearded kings and ibis-crowned queens; sometimes they were images of children.

===Chinese===
“这几位显赫的远客想看看托勒密诸王的葬地，”卡莱布说。“他们是王公贵人，想必也对亚历山大大帝的陵寝感兴趣。”

“死亡不过是一场沉睡，是通往永恒阳光殿堂的暮色过渡，”负责的祭司答道。“尘世的伟业，是登上俄西里斯（Osiris）不朽宫殿的易朽台阶；我们死去的君主如今环坐他左右，登位称尊，头上环绕着普申特（pschent）双冠，手中握着圣甲虫权杖。伟大的伊西斯已向他们显现为真理的光辉，因为为了他们的极乐，她揭起了面纱，让他们得见真容。人生不过一场梦，死是一座桥，而永恒才是生。”

卡莱布迈着碎步走在前面，红色马靴的靴尖点着地，一样样指点；老祭司则继续吟诵那些永恒的真理，仿佛自言自语。花岗岩、斑岩和大理石的陵墓，镌着象形文字，像神庙一样呈金字塔形耸起。祭司这时走到旅人们前面，下了几级台阶：里面，在地下墓室看不见的深处，木乃伊们安息在彩绘的石棺里；立灯在三脚架上燃烧，香烟从瓶盘里腾起，结成一团云雾；色彩艳丽的小玻璃器皿盛着油、蜂蜜和果子，摆放在低矮的青铜桌上，而盛着圣水的大双耳瓶静候复活的时辰——到那时死者将起身，受洗进入真正的新生，那便是永恒。甜腻刺鼻的香料气味浓得逼人；在香烟的雾霭里，棺盖上彩绘像那双睁得大大的眼睛，幽灵般、超凡脱俗地直望着前方渐亮的未来。那些像有蓄须的王，有戴朱鹭冠的后；有时是孩童的像。

===Original===
Through the mist of the aromatics the golden, winged suns gleamed in the embrace of the snakes coiled tail in mouth. Sacred Horus, son of Osiris and Isis, the radiant redeemer of mankind, who descended out of pity on a sinful world, bestrode Typhon, the grinning spirit of evil. There were images of the god Apis, of the god Râ, of Thoth and Anubis, with the heads of an ox, an ibis, a dog.

After this, the shade of the sycamores and tamarisks outside the tombs was silver-green and cool; and the pure air of the sunny morning seemed strange after the perfumed, sickly-sweet atmosphere of the sultry underground sepulchres. The priest in charge stopped before a gleaming marble pyramid. The narrow bronze door hung tapering upwards between pilasters carved with lotus-capitals.

“The tomb of Alexander of Macedon,” said the custodian, solemnly.

They went inside. Again, burning lamps shed their fragrance. There was a heavy mist of nard. Behind a bronze railing on a basalt pedestal stood a sarcophagus of transparent crystal, polished and engraved. And within this thick crystal, in a green watery light, where the flame of the lamps was mirrored in the glass, a mummy lay visible. It was like the chrysalis of a gigantic moth. The face was stained brown with balsam and salve and stared with eyes of beryl. The hair and short beard were painted gold. Many-coloured bandages wrapped the body in a close sheath; and the legs also were closely fastened together in a case of gold filagree.

The mummy lay on a mattress of striped byssus, the head on a byssus pillow. The scarlet lips seemed to grin in the crisp golden beard and the beryl eyes were full of amazement at what they saw in eternity.

===Chinese===
透过香料的雾霭，金色的带翼太阳在衔尾相缠的蛇的怀抱里闪光。神圣的荷鲁斯（Horus），俄西里斯与伊西斯之子，人类光辉的救主，出于怜悯降临有罪的人世，骑跨在堤丰（Typhon）——那咧嘴狞笑的恶灵——身上。还有众神的像：阿皮斯（Apis）、拉（Râ）、托特（Thoth）和阿努比斯（Anubis），长着公牛、朱鹭和狗的头。

此后，陵外西克莫无花果树和柽柳的浓荫是银绿色、清凉的；在地下墓室那香气弥漫、甜腻闷热的空气之后，晴朗早晨的纯净空气显得异样。负责的祭司在一座闪亮的大理石金字塔前停住。窄窄的青铜门向上收窄，立在雕着莲花柱头的壁柱之间。

“马其顿的亚历山大之墓，”司阍郑重地说。

他们走了进去。又是燃烧的灯洒着芬芳。甘松香的浓雾沉沉。玄武岩基座上、青铜栏杆后面，立着一具透明水晶的石棺，打磨光亮，刻着花纹。在这厚厚的水晶里，在一泓泛绿的水光中——灯焰映在晶壁上——躺着一具看得见的木乃伊。它像一只巨大蛾子的蛹。脸被香膏药膏染成褐色，一双绿宝石的眼睛凝视着。头发和短须涂成金色。多彩的绷带把身体裹成紧窄的一鞘；双腿也紧紧并拢，套在一个金丝编的套子里。

木乃伊躺在条纹细麻绢（byssus）的褥垫上，头枕着细麻绢枕头。赤红的嘴唇在金色硬须里似乎咧着笑，绿宝石似的眼睛里满是惊异，惊异它在永恒中所见到的一切。

===Original===
“These are the sacred remains of the great Alexander,” said the priest in charge. “History teaches us that Ptolemy, son of Lagus, took the body of the hero and conqueror from Perdiccas, who was bringing it back from Babylon to Macedon, but was passing through Egypt in the hope of conquering our sacred country. Ptolemy marched against him; Perdiccas had hardly set foot in Egypt when he perished at the hands of his own soldiery on an island which had been surrounded by Ptolemy’s troops. With Perdiccas were the royal family: Alexander’s pregnant widow Roxana and her young children. They were allowed to embark for Macedon, but the body of Alexander the Great was carried to Alexandria and buried in state in a massive gold sarcophagus. This sarcophagus was stolen by Ptolemy Parisactus, a pretender to the Egyptian throne, who invaded the country from Syria with a host of troops. Alexander’s body, however, was rescued from his hands and laid in this crystal coffin. Here it lies.”

Lucius and his companions stared, greatly moved by the sight of this corpse nearly three centuries old, embalmed and bandaged, with its feet in a sheath of gold filagree and its beryl eyes staring with surprise. Was this chrysalis all that remained of the great Alexander, whom the oracle of Ammon had declared to be the son of Ammon-Râ son of the sun-god?

Only Caleb remained indifferent, with his mincing gait and an incredulous little laugh at the genuineness of Alexander’s body, to which he had already conducted so many “princely nobles,” including Kardusi of Persia and Baabab of Mesopotamia.

“Here lies Alexander the Great,” continued the priest in charge. “The warrior, the conqueror, the king of kings, the son of the sacred sun-god, Ammon-Râ, descended upon earth. He lived to be thirty-three in this terrestrial life. But this life is a dream and death is the bridge to the life that is the eternal reality. The soul has departed from this house embalmed with precious ointments. …”

===Chinese===
“这些便是伟大的亚历山大的圣骸，”负责的祭司说。“史书教导我们：托勒密，拉古斯之子，从佩尔狄卡斯（Perdiccas）手里夺走了这位英雄与征服者的遗体；佩尔狄卡斯正把遗体从巴比伦运回马其顿，途经埃及，指望征服我们这个神圣的国度。托勒密进军迎击；佩尔狄卡斯刚踏上埃及的土地，就在一座已被托勒密军团围住的岛上死于自己部下之手。与佩尔狄卡斯同行的还有王室：亚历山大怀着遗腹子的寡妇罗克珊娜（Roxana）和她的年幼孩子们。他们获准登船回马其顿，而亚历山大大帝的遗体被运到亚历山大里亚，以帝王之礼安葬在一具巨大的黄金石棺里。那具石棺后来被托勒密·帕里萨克图斯（Ptolemy Parisactus）盗走——他是个觊觎埃及王位的人，从叙利亚领着大军入侵埃及。但亚历山大的遗体从他手中被夺回，安放进这具水晶棺。它就在这里。”

卢基乌斯和同伴们凝望着，被眼前的景象深深震动：这具近三百年高龄的遗体，涂过香膏，缠着绷带，双脚套在金丝鞘里，绿宝石的眼睛惊讶地瞪视着。难道伟大的亚历山大剩下的就只是这只蛹了吗？阿蒙的神谕不是宣布过，他是阿蒙-拉（Ammon-Râ）之子，而阿蒙-拉乃太阳神之子吗？

只有卡莱布无动于衷，迈着他的碎步，对亚历山大遗体的真伪露出一声将信将疑的轻笑——他已经领过太多“王公贵人”来看过它了，包括波斯的卡尔杜西和美索不达米亚的巴阿巴布。

“这里躺着亚历山大大帝，”负责的祭司继续说。“战士，征服者，万王之王，神圣太阳神阿蒙-拉降临人间的儿子。他在尘世的生命活到三十三岁。但这生命是一场梦，死亡是通往那生命——永恒的实在——的桥。灵魂已经离开这座用贵重香膏涂敷的屋宇。……”

===Original===
And he added, in a different voice:

“Even to your excellencies the charge is only one gold stater a head. …”

“I will pay for you, my lord!” smiled Caleb, with an elegant bow to Lucius.

And he paid the priest, who went on speaking, with the gold coins shining in his uplifted hand:

“Generosity is a great virtue. He who gives more than he is asked to give earns the favour of Thoth, who sews the good chances of fortune upon the earth.”

Caleb grinned with flashing teeth to show that he understood and dropped another half-stater into the priest’s palm.

===Chinese===
接着他换了一种嗓音，补充道：

“便是诸位大人，每位也只收一个金斯塔特（stater）。……”

“我来替您付，老爷！”卡莱布向卢基乌斯优雅一躬，笑着说。

他付了钱；祭司举着手，金币在掌中闪光，嘴里继续说着：

“慷慨是大德。肯给多于所索的人，赢得托特的恩宠；是托特把命运的好机缘缝到大地上。”

卡莱布亮着牙齿咧嘴一笑，表示心领神会，又往祭司掌心丢下半个斯塔特。

===Original===
The travellers stepped out of the sepulchre. The sunny morning outside seemed strange, with silver-green shadows between waving tamarisks and rustling sycamore-leaves.

Lucius was pale. And he said to Thrasyllus and Uncle Catullus:

“Death! … Death! … She is perhaps dead. … She is drowned, perhaps, in the sea … and we shall never recover her exquisite body, to embalm it. …”

“In any case she has disappeared, my dear nephew,” said Uncle Catullus, trying to console him. “Let us think of her no more. By all the gods, try to forget her: she had thick ankles and large feet. … Lucius, do be sensible at last! Enjoy yourself during this interesting tour. We have had a morning more interesting than any that we ever had at Rome. We have seen an ideal system of scavenging, we have heard philosophical and religious truths and we have seen the mummy of Alexander! I’ve really received too many new impressions. My brains are soaked like an overfull sponge: they can contain no more this morning. That sated condition of the head makes my stomach feel empty, as empty as my pocket when your liberality has forgotten to line it for your old uncle. My dear Lucius, when travelling one must be sparing … of one’s powers. I suggest to our indefatigable guide that we should go home and see if, in our absence, our trusty cook has remembered that, though life is a dream, even the dead and therefore all the more the living have to be fed. The dead are sustained with oil, honey and fruit: I am curious to see what our cook’s pious thoughts have prepared today for the living.”

===Chinese===
旅人们走出墓室。外面晴朗的早晨显得异样，柽柳摇曳、西克莫无花果叶沙沙作响，其间是银绿色的浓荫。

卢基乌斯脸色发白。他对特拉叙卢斯和卡图卢斯叔父说：

“死！……死！……她也许死了。……也许淹死在海里了……我们永远寻不回她那美妙的身子，没法给她涂香防腐了。……” 

“反正她是失踪了，亲爱的侄儿，”卡图卢斯叔父竭力安慰他说。“咱们别再想她了。凭众神起誓，想法子忘掉她吧：她脚踝粗、脚又大。……卢基乌斯，你总算讲点道理吧！在这趟有趣的游览里好好享受享受。我们过了个早晨，比在罗马哪天都有意思。我们见识了一套理想的清道办法，听了哲学与宗教的真理，还看了亚历山大的木乃伊！我领受的新印象实在太多了。我的脑子像吸饱了的海绵：今天上午再也装不下了。脑子这样饱，倒叫我觉得肚子空了，就像您的慷慨忘了给您老叔父的口袋垫上点什么的时候，我的口袋一样空空如也。亲爱的卢基乌斯，出门在外，凡事得省着点……省着自己的力气。我向咱们这位不知疲倦的向导提议：回家去，瞧瞧咱们不在的时候，咱们那位可靠的厨子还记不记得——虽说人生是一场梦，可连死人都得吃饭，活人就更不用说了。死人靠油、蜜和果子供养：我倒好奇，咱们的厨子今天怀着怎样的虔敬心思，给活人备下了什么。”

===Original===
The procession trotted home through the gardens of Bruchium, the palace quarter, and along the Hippodrome to Master Ghizla’s great diversorium, or guesthouse. It stood near the Canopian gate in an oval garden, behind a hedge of tall cactuses; the door opened between two figures of Hermes. Here sat the janitor, or porter; and the travellers were struck by the fact that a winged head of Hermes, in marble, crowned the marble architrave of the door. Caducei, or Hermes’-wands, with winding snakes, were carved on the pilasters of the door; for the diversorium was dedicated to Hermes and known in the quarter as the Hermes House.

The janitor rose and bowed, with his hands stretched to the ground. Master Ghizla also, standing beside a statue of Hermes in the middle of his garden, bowed in this fashion, bending low, with his hands stretched groundwards.

The procession trotted in, the travellers alighted, but Caleb sat his mare, bowed gracefully and, stooping forward, whispered in Lucius’ ear:

“After you have rested, my lord, I will take you whither you please, I will procure you whatsoever you please … for your lordship’s pleasure and gratification. Whithersoever you please and whatsoever you please. … I wish you good luck at your repast.”

===Chinese===
队伍小跑着回家，穿过布鲁基乌姆的园林——王宫区——沿着竞技场，来到吉兹拉老板的大客栈。客栈坐落在卡诺皮斯门（Canopian Gate）附近一座椭圆的花园里，藏在一道高高的仙人掌篱后面；大门开在两尊赫尔墨斯（Hermes）像之间。看门人坐在这里；旅人们注意到，门上大理石门楣的顶端还有一颗带翼的赫尔墨斯头像，也是大理石的。门柱上雕着双蛇缠绕的节杖——赫尔墨斯神杖；因为这客栈奉献给赫尔墨斯，坊间都管它叫赫尔墨斯之家。

看门人起身行礼，双手直伸到地。吉兹拉老板也站在园子中央一尊赫尔墨斯像旁，以同样的姿势行礼：深深弯腰，双手向地伸展。

队伍小跑着进门，旅人们下了肩舆；卡莱布却骑坐在母马上，优雅地一躬身，俯身向前，在卢基乌斯耳边低语：

“您歇过之后，老爷，您想去哪儿我就带您去哪儿，您想要什么我就给您弄什么……全凭老爷开心取乐。随便哪儿，随便什么。……祝您用餐愉快。”

===Original===
With that he threw the mare on her haunches, stood up in the stirrups, waved his burnous, uttered a cry and rode away, in a cloud of graceful gestures.

The diversorium consisted of several low buildings. It harboured Arabian and Phoenician merchants, who looked out curiously, squatting on mats or lying at their meal, served by black slaves. But Master Ghizla led his “princely guests” to their own suites; and Vettius and Rufus received the travellers on the threshold. They had worked to good purpose, conveying furniture, boxes and packing-cases on camels and mules. A Babylonian carpet lay upon the floor; the travellers’ own beds were ready; in the corners of Lucius’ bedroom stood bronze and marble statues, for no important Roman with any pretension to taste travelled without carrying a few of his treasures with him; and perfumes burned before the statues. There were curtains hanging from rings; and garments lay ready, neatly folded and strewn with fragrant flowers, on long, low, sycamore-wood tables. There were metal mirrors on bronze pedestals; all the brushes, tweezers and unguent-sticks, in gold adorned with agates, lay spread on bronze tables; all the jars, pots and vases essential to the toilet stood filled with cosmetics, ointments and perfumes. All this furniture and upholstery, all these useful and artistic possessions had been brought over from the ship.

“My diversorium boasts every possible comfort, my lord, and all the latest conveniences,” bragged Ghizla, “which visitors like your lordship demand in these days.”

He lifted a curtain beside Lucius’ couch: there was in fact a marble basin with taps, under a canopy.

“And here,” said Master Ghizla, “is your triclinium.”

===Chinese===
说罢他猛地一收缰，母马后坐蹲下；他站上马镫，挥动布努斯，喊了一声，驰马而去，撒下一路优雅的手势，如一团云。

客栈由几座低矮的屋宇组成。里头住着阿拉伯和腓尼基商人，蹲在席子上，或躺着用餐，由黑奴伺候，好奇地向外张望。但吉兹拉老板把他的“王公贵客”领到他们自己的套院；维提乌斯和鲁弗斯在门槛前迎候旅人。他们干活很见成效，用骆驼和骡子把家具、箱笼都搬运安置妥当。地板上铺着一条巴比伦地毯；旅人自家的卧榻已经备好；卢基乌斯卧房的角落里立着青铜和大理石的雕像——凡稍有身份、稍讲品味的罗马人出门，无不随身携带几件自己的珍藏；像前燃着香。帷幔挂在环扣上；衣裳叠得整整齐齐、撒着香花，摆放在长长的矮矮的西克莫无花果木桌上。青铜座架上支着金属镜子；所有的刷子、镊子、香膏签，金质镶着玛瑙，摊放在青铜桌上；梳妆必需的罐、盒、瓶，一概盛着脂粉、油膏和香露。这些家具陈设，这些又实用又艺术的物件，全都是从船上运过来的。

“我的客栈什么舒适都有，老爷，还有各式最新的便利，”吉兹拉夸口道，“如今像您老爷这样的客人要的就是这些。”

他掀起卢基乌斯卧榻旁的一幅帷幔：华盖底下果然有一座带龙头的大理石水盆。

“这儿，”吉兹拉老板说，“是您的躺餐厅（triclinium）。”

===Original===
The dining-room which Master Ghizla described by this high-sounding name was a pleasant, spacious, airy apartment, with sun-blinds between pillars; and, as Lucius entered, he was greeted with the music of harps. For all the wealthy young Roman’s “family” were drawn up there in two rows, awaiting his arrival: Vettius and Rufus and Tarrar, the little black slave; all his slaves, male and female, all the great household without which no distinguished Roman thought it possible to live, even—indeed especially—when travelling. And, amid the female slaves, stood the Greek slave from Cos, Cora, with two other harpists; and they drew long, descending cords from their strings, while Cora sang a short song of welcome to the gracious master. Incense burned on dishes; two S-shaped couches coiled round a long, low table covered with a yellow-and-white cloth and already laid with yellow-and-white crockery and gleaming gold plate. A little fountain of verbena-water played in the middle of a bowl filled with blue lotus.

Lucius assured Vettius and Rufus that he was really pleased; indeed it was as though he were at home. Then, because Uncle Catullus said that he was starving, he invited his uncle and parasite, who had so often diverted him with a merry jest, to lie down, lay down himself and motioned Thrasyllus, his friend and tutor, to a stool by his side, for, though Thrasyllus shared his pupil’s meals, as a freedman he remained the inferior and ate seated. Tarrar and three girl-slaves waited, while Cora and the two harpists struck a soft melody from their strings or danced a little ballet.

Uncle Catullus was glad to have neither oysters nor roast peacock set before him: Lucius’ cook had surpassed himself, in this first exotic repast, with a first course of peppered watermelon in sugared wine-sauce, with which was served an Egyptian spiced bread, named caces; next, young tunnies, surrounded by savoury eggs, stuffed olives and finely chopped coxcombs; next, a sucking-pig served on breadfruit and cucumbers; lastly, a honey-tart, covered with a cream custard containing stoned dates and cinnamon. They had the celebrated Mareotis wine, thick as ink and purple as molten wax, poured by Master Ghizla himself out of a jar still warm from the sun; and there was the topaz-yellow Ethiopian liqueur of Napata, which he dripped drop by drop into goblets filled with snow and which spread an aroma as of roses steeped in silphium.

Uncle Catullus ate his fill and Lucius too did honour to the meal, however much his heart still suffered and craved, while Thrasyllus was moderate as always. Then a legitimate drowsiness overcame the three travellers and they withdrew behind their curtains, to rest.

===Chinese===
吉兹拉老板用这个响亮名号称呼的餐厅，是一间宜人、宽敞、透气的屋子，柱间挂着遮阳帘；卢基乌斯一进门，迎接他的便是竖琴之乐。因为这位阔绰的罗马青年全部的“家眷”已在两排侍立，等候他驾临：维提乌斯、鲁弗斯和小黑奴塔拉尔；他所有的男女奴隶、整个庞大的家务班底——没有它，任何有身份的罗马人都认为没法过日子，哪怕出门在外，不，尤其在出门在外时。女奴中间立着科斯来的希腊女奴科拉，还有另外两名竖琴女；她们从琴弦上拨出长长下行的和弦，科拉唱了一支短歌，欢迎仁慈的主人。盘里燃着香；两张 S 形卧榻盘绕着一张铺黄白桌布的长矮桌，桌上已摆好黄白的陶器和闪亮的金餐具。一只盛满蓝色睡莲的大盆中央，一小股马鞭草香水的喷泉在轻轻喷洒。

卢基乌斯向维提乌斯和鲁弗斯表示他确实满意；真的，简直像在自己家里一样。随后，因为卡图卢斯叔父说自己快饿死了，他便邀请他的叔父兼清客——这位常以妙语替他解闷——躺下，自己也躺下，又示意他的朋友兼教师特拉叙卢斯坐到身旁的凳子上；因为特拉叙卢斯虽与学生同席，身为获释奴仍是卑者，要坐着吃。塔拉尔和三个小女奴侍候着，科拉和两名竖琴女时而从弦上拨出轻柔的旋律，时而跳上一小段舞。

卡图卢斯叔父很高兴眼前的菜里既没有牡蛎也没有烤孔雀：卢基乌斯的厨子在这第一顿异乡风味餐里超水平发挥：头一道是糖酒汁浸的撒胡椒的西瓜，配着一种名叫卡凯斯（caces）的埃及香料面包；接着是小金枪鱼，四周围着咸香的蛋、酿橄榄和剁碎的鸡冠；再下一道是奶猪，衬着面包果和黄瓜；最后是蜜馅饼，盖着一层奶冻，里头嵌着去核的椰枣和肉桂。他们喝的是著名的马雷奥蒂斯酒——稠得像墨、紫得像熔蜡——由吉兹拉老板亲手从一只还带着阳光余温的坛子里斟出；还有纳帕塔黄玉色的埃塞俄比亚利口酒，他一滴一滴地滴进盛满雪的高脚杯里，那酒散出一股香气，像玫瑰浸过罗盘草。

卡图卢斯叔父吃了个心满意足；卢基乌斯尽管心里还在受苦、还在渴念，也给这顿饭撑足了场面；特拉叙卢斯则一如既往地节制。随后，一阵理直气壮的困意袭上三位旅人，他们退到各自的帷幔后面歇息去了。

## IV / 四

===Original===
But Lucius did not sleep. Now that he was alone, he felt the agony of his suffering and affliction. He drew a sandal from a little casket, a woman’s blue-leather sandal adorned with gold relief and small, for all that Uncle Catullus was pleased to say. It was the only trace that Ilia had left behind her. And he kissed the sandal and groaned and stretched himself out impotently and clenched his fists and lay like that, staring before him without moving.

He lay lost in thought. And suddenly he struck the gong and summoned Tarrar, who entered nimbly and respectfully:

“Find Caleb and bring him here to me.”

The little slave returned in a short time and ushered in Caleb, who approached with graceful salaams. Tarrar left his master alone with the Sabaean.

“Caleb,” said Lucius, “sit down and listen. I need your advice.”

“Your faithful servant is listening, my lord,” said Caleb, sitting down on a chair.

“Caleb,” continued Lucius, “I have not come to Egypt merely to see the things of interest which this country supplies. I have another object. There are mysterious oracles in Egypt; there are prophets and sibyls, so I am told, living in the desert. I want to know something. I want to know where someone is, one who is dear to me and far away. I want to consult the oracles and the prophets and sibyls. You must conduct me, without saying a word to my uncle or my tutor, because they do not approve of the attempts which I wish to make to find this person whom I love. Be my guide, Caleb, and I will reward you.”

===Chinese===
卢基乌斯却没有睡。如今独自一人，他尝尽了痛苦煎熬的滋味。他从一只小匣里取出一只凉鞋，一只蓝皮革的女式凉鞋，饰着金浮雕，小小的——随卡图卢斯叔父爱怎么说去吧。这是伊利娅留下的唯一痕迹。他吻着那只凉鞋，呻吟着，无可奈何地伸展四肢，攥紧拳头，就那样躺着，一动不动地瞪视着前方。

他躺着，陷在沉思里。突然他敲响铜锣，唤来塔拉尔；小奴麻利而恭顺地进来：

“去找卡莱布，把他带到我这儿来。”

小奴不多时便回来了，引进卡莱布；卡莱布一路打着优雅的躬身礼走近前来。塔拉尔把主人单独留给了那位萨巴人。

“卡莱布，”卢基乌斯说，“坐下，听着。我需要你的主意。”

“您忠心的仆人听着呢，老爷，”卡莱布说着，在一张椅子上坐下。

“卡莱布，”卢基乌斯接着说，“我到埃及来，不只是为了看这个国家供给的种种有趣东西。我另有目的。埃及有神秘的谕示；听说沙漠里还住着先知和女巫。我想知道一件事。我想知道一个人在哪里，一个离我遥远、为我所爱的人。我要去请教那些谕示、先知和女巫。你必须带我去，一个字也不要对我叔父或我教师讲，因为他们不赞成我想方设法去寻这位我所爱的人。做我的向导吧，卡莱布，我会赏你。”

===Original===
